# The reverse engineering band

The programme map splits the 139 nodes closest to the alleged programme into seven bands, and this file is the reverse engineering band: 16 nodes, taken era by era from the earliest to the most recent. Each entry gives what the node is, why the board places it here, why it holds the tier it holds, what it has denied, its documented and testimonial claims with their sources, and the links that tie it to the rest of the board.

Names in full, for this file:

- **AAWSAP**: Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program

# THROUGH 1969: Estimate through Blue Book

## Defense Intelligence Agency

Defense Intelligence Agency is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1961; axes C3 A3 D1; 35 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Combat support agency; AAWSAP/AATIP executive agent.

### What it is

The Defense Intelligence Agency is the Department of Defense combat support agency responsible for foreign military intelligence. Between 2008 and 2012 it managed the Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program, the congressionally earmarked effort that produced thirty-eight Defense Intelligence Reference Documents on advanced propulsion, materials and related subjects, executed under contract by Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies. AARO has confirmed that the material generated by that programme remains in DIA's archived holdings.

### Why it is implicated

DIA is the only agency the United States Government has formally admitted ran a modern programme that commissioned studies on how one would build and detect craft with capabilities no terrestrial system possesses, and it is the acknowledged custodian of that archive today. Its programme manager approved a contractor's UAP field investigations that were not in the statement of work, including proposals to stand up laboratories to examine any recovered UFO materials, which is a documented plan for a materials exploitation capability inside a defence intelligence contract. When the Senate Majority Leader asked in June 2009 that the programme be raised to Special Access Program status, the Department refused; when DIA cancelled it, its participants immediately proposed reconstituting it under Homeland Security as a compartment explicitly justified by claims that relevant material existed. AARO's own explanation for the entire modern disclosure movement traces it back to this DIA programme and its people. DIA is therefore the acknowledged root of the modern record, the custodian of its archive, and absent from the National Archives UAP collection.

### The longer account

In 2008 DIA posted a solicitation and awarded a contract to Bigelow Aerospace for what became the Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program. The money came from Senate-directed appropriations rather than a Departmental requirement, a point the DoD Inspector General records plainly when it notes that the Department did not officially look at UAP again after Blue Book until select members of Congress initiated and funded a programme under DIA's direction. The programme ran from 2008 through 2012 in its various forms and produced thirty-eight Defense Intelligence Reference Documents covering advanced propulsion, warp drive metrics, traversable wormholes, metamaterials, invisibility cloaking, high energy laser weapons, and biological effects of anomalous exposure. DIA transmitted the list of titles and principal authors to Congress in January 2018.

AARO's own account of AAWSAP, in Historical Record Report Volume I and in its standalone KONA BLUE paper, is more useful to this board than AARO seems to have realised. It confirms the programme was managed by DIA from 2009 to 2012 and funded through congressional earmarks with Bigelow Aerospace as prime contractor. It confirms the stated purpose was investigating next-generation aerospace technologies across twelve specific areas including advanced lift, propulsion, unconventional materials and controls, and signature reduction. It then states that although investigating UFO or UAP was not specifically outlined in the contract's statement of work, the selected private sector organisation conducted UFO research with the support of the DIA programme manager, and that this research included reviewing new cases and older Project BLUE BOOK cases, operating debriefing and investigatory teams, and proposals to set up laboratories to examine any recovered UFO materials.

That sentence is the whole argument in miniature. A defence intelligence agency programme, funded outside the normal requirements process, with a programme manager who authorised his contractor to run field investigation teams and to propose materials exploitation laboratories for recovered UFO material. AARO's framing is that DIA did not seek nor specifically authorise this work though a DIA employee set up and managed the contract, which is an attempt to place the agency at arm's length from its own programme manager. It does not work. A contract managed by an agency employee, funded by an agency line, delivering to an agency archive, is the agency's programme.

The escalation attempt is documented. On 24 June 2009 Senator Harry Reid wrote to Deputy Secretary of Defense William Lynn III requesting that AAWSAP/AATIP be made a DoD Special Access Program. Lynn declined on the recommendation of then Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence James Clapper, who advised that the designation was not justified. Read against the concealment thesis, a request for SAP status is a request for a compartment in which recovered material could lawfully be held without disclosure, and the refusal is the point at which the effort had to find another home.

It found one, briefly. AARO's KONA BLUE paper states that when DIA cancelled AAWSAP/AATIP, several individuals involved advocated that the Department of Homeland Security take the effort over and fund a new version under the code name KONA BLUE. In 2011 the DHS Under Secretary for Science and Technology established KONA BLUE as a Prospective Special Access Program based on claims that relevant information and material existed and required this level of protection. Six months later the Deputy Secretary of DHS disapproved it and directed immediate termination. AARO's insistence that no data or material was ever transferred to DHS is not the interesting part. The interesting part is that a Presidentially appointed, Senate-confirmed Under Secretary stood up a prospective compartment on the strength of a representation that material existed.

The custody question is settled by AARO's own words. Its KONA BLUE paper states that information associated with the activities conducted under the auspices of AAWSAP/AATIP remains within DIA's archived holdings. Whatever the thirty-eight reference documents, the field investigation files, the debriefing records and the case reviews amount to, DIA has them. That makes DIA a custody node on this map regardless of what one concludes about the underlying phenomenon.

The people matter. James T. Lacatski, a career DIA propulsion and missile intelligence specialist, was the programme manager who brought the subject to Senator Reid and who converted the contractor's deliverables into Defense Intelligence Reference Documents. He co-authored Skinwalkers at the Pentagon with Colm Kelleher, the biochemist who ran the contractor's field science effort, and George Knapp, the investigative reporter who first obtained and published the DIRD title list. AARO's Volume I addresses that body of work by describing the contractor's Utah property investigations, its interest in shadow figures, creatures, remote viewing and human consciousness anomalies, and its plan to hire psychics. AARO presents this as evidence the programme lacked merit. It is equally evidence that a defence intelligence agency programme was pursuing lines of inquiry it would never have funded without something driving it.

One further fact belongs on the board. AARO states that it partnered with DIA's archives for the historical review. Yet as of the National Archives Record Group 615 page's review date of 22 May 2026, DIA does not appear among the agencies with a UAP records transmittal to the National Archives. The agency that holds the acknowledged archive of the acknowledged programme has not, on the public record, delivered it to the statutory collection.

### What it has denied

DIA has not issued a standalone public denial of the legacy programme allegation. Its position is expressed through AARO's account, which is that DIA terminated AAWSAP/AATIP for lack of merit and lack of utility, that DIA neither sought nor authorised the contractor's UFO field research, and that no material of any kind was transferred to DHS under KONA BLUE. DIA has released the thirty-eight Defense Intelligence Reference Documents and confirmed the AAWSAP record sits in its archived holdings.

Roles the board assigns it: funding, custody, reverse-engineering, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- DIA has not, on the public record, transmitted its UAP records to the National Archives Record Group 615, despite holding the acknowledged AAWSAP/AATIP archive and despite the FY2024 NDAA transfer requirement.
- The full AAWSAP contract file, including the statement of work, the modification history and the deliverable acceptance record, has not been released in unredacted form.
- AARO says a DIA employee set up and managed the contract without the agency seeking or authorising the UFO field work. Which DIA official approved the contract, and under what internal authority, is not documented publicly.
- The contractor's proposal to establish laboratories to examine recovered UFO materials is referenced in Volume I but the proposal itself has not been released, and it is not known whether any such laboratory capability was stood up or transferred.
- The claim in Skinwalkers at the Pentagon regarding what the DIA programme manager was personally shown has not been corroborated by any released document and is not asserted on this board.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 7 of 1066, score 151). 35 connections, 19 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 5 official denials recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D1.

## 'Manhattan Project 2.0' (alleged secrecy architecture)

'Manhattan Project 2.0' (alleged secrecy architecture) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1947; axes C2 A3 D3; 10 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Alleged 1947-present secrecy construct.

### What it is

An alleged secrecy and industrial architecture -- not an established one -- in which a legacy U.S. materials-retrieval and reverse-engineering effort is said to have been run since the 1940s on the organisational model of the Manhattan Project: extreme compartmentalisation, industrial-scale contractor execution, and funding and reporting arrangements that keep the effort outside normal congressional visibility. The label is used descriptively by named proponents, chiefly retired Army Colonel Karl E. Nell, who has publicly called for an open 'Manhattan project' to reverse engineer recovered UAP precisely because he asserts a closed one already exists.

### Why it is implicated

This is the organising hypothesis that makes the rest of the board cohere, and it must be tiered honestly: the architecture's existence is a Tier 3 allegation carried by named, credentialed proponents, not a documented fact. What raises it above rumour is the specificity and the seniority of who says it. Nell was the Army's liaison officer to the UAP Task Force from 2021 to 2022 and later Modernization Advisor to the Vice Chief of Staff of the Army; he asserted on the record that an eighty-year sub-rosa arms race focused on reverse engineering technologies of unknown origin is 'fundamentally correct,' and he presented a dated campaign plan with an October 1, 2030 disclosure target and the explicit objective of restoring 'proper Federal government oversight over all UAP legacy (and ongoing) program efforts.' A serving-adjacent officer does not build a slide deck to restore oversight over programs he believes are imaginary. The Department's own rebuttal, meanwhile, concedes the shape of the thing while denying its content: AARO acknowledges a prospective DHS special access program built to protect the retrieval and exploitation of 'non-human biologics,' and a 2021 IC controlled access program expanded without sufficient justification to protect UAP reverse-engineering.

### The longer account

The name is an analogy, and the analogy is doing analytical work, so it is worth stating precisely what is being claimed and what is not.

What is not claimed: that a document exists bearing the title 'Manhattan Project II.' No such document has been produced. The phrase circulates in independent investigative work and podcasting, and its use there does not constitute evidence.

What is claimed, by named individuals with attributable positions: that the United States has run, since roughly the 1940s, a compartmented effort to recover and exploit materials of non-human origin, structured to be invisible to ordinary oversight, and that the Manhattan Project is the correct organisational template for understanding it. The template matters because the Manhattan Project is the canonical American precedent for a state-scale industrial programme conducted in total secrecy -- AARO's own historical review notes that in 1942 'the USG started the Manhattan Project. This program operated in secrecy and led to the establishment of several "secret cities" across the United States to support the development of the nuclear bomb.' That is the model: not a hidden filing cabinet but a hidden industrial base.

Karl E. Nell is the central named proponent. At the first symposium of the Sol Foundation, held at Stanford in November 2023, Nell called for a 'campaign plan' to force greater transparency and a 'Manhattan project' to reverse engineer recovered UAP. His presented slides set a disclosure target of October 1, 2030, acknowledged schedule risk, and listed the intended outcomes: 'Proper Oversight Restored,' 'Catastrophic Disclosure Avoid[ed],' and 'Scientific Understanding Advanced.' The operative line is his advocacy for 'restoration of proper Federal government oversight over all UAP legacy (and ongoing) program efforts.' The word 'restoration' presupposes that oversight was lost. The words 'legacy (and ongoing)' presuppose a continuous programme lineage.

Nell's credentials are not in dispute. He was the Army's liaison for the UAP Task Force from 2021 to 2022, working alongside Grusch. Lieutenant General Laura A. Potter, Deputy Chief of Staff for Intelligence, Headquarters, Department of the Army, described him in a 2022 performance evaluation as 'an officer with the strongest possible moral compass.' His most recent listed position was Modernization Advisor to the Vice Chief of Staff of the Army.

The second proponent is Hal Puthoff, the former CIA-affiliated physicist, who told the same Stanford symposium that in 2004 a CIA think tank, the Defense Intelligence Agency and the Pentagon broadly agreed that information about UFOs should not be declassified, deeming the societal risks too great. This is a reported claim about an unproduced 2004 decision, and it is tiered accordingly -- but note its structural role: it supplies a mechanism and a date for the maintenance of secrecy in the modern era rather than its origin.

The third is Grusch, who framed the same object financially and temporally: a 'publicly unknown Cold War for recovered and exploited physical material,' an 'eighty-year arms race' whose continuation in secrecy 'further inhibits the world populace to be prepared for an unexpected, non-human intelligence contact scenario.' The fourth is Jonathan Grey of the National Air and Space Intelligence Center at Wright-Patterson, who said on the record that 'the existence of complex historical programs involving the coordinated retrieval and study of exotic materials, dating back to the early 20th century, should no longer remain a secret.' The fifth is Christopher Mellon, who said officials had shared with him 'insights into the history, governing documents and the location where a craft was allegedly abandoned and recovered' -- governing documents being the phrase an analyst should mark.

The Department's counter-narrative is the KONA BLUE file, and it deserves close reading because it concedes more than it intends. AARO reports that multiple interviewees identified KONA BLUE as 'a Department of Homeland Security (DHS) sensitive compartment established to protect the retrieval and exploitation of "non-human biologics."' AARO's correction is that KONA BLUE was a Prospective Special Access Program proposed to DHS leadership, never approved, never funded, never in receipt of material. But AARO also states that in 2011 the DHS Under Secretary for Science and Technology did establish KONA BLUE as a PSAP, citing claims that relevant information and material existed and required that level of protection, and citing congressional interest and possible homeland security impacts; and that six months later the Deputy Secretary of DHS disapproved it as a SAP and directed immediate termination over the adequacy of justification and the sufficiency of information including personnel and budget requirements. So: a Cabinet department stood up a protective compartment on the represented existence of retrieved non-human material, and a Deputy Secretary killed it. That is an architecture attempting to form and being caught mid-formation.

AARO traces the proposal's lineage to AAWSAP/AATIP, managed by DIA from 2009 to 2012, funded through congressional earmarks, with Bigelow Aerospace of Nevada as prime contractor. And AARO separately concedes: 'In 2021, without sufficient justification, the scope of an IC Controlled Access Program was expanded to protect UAP reverse-engineering.'

AARO's overall verdict is flat: 'AARO found no empirical evidence for claims that the USG and private companies have been reverse-engineering extraterrestrial technology,' the narrative being 'in large part, the result of circular reporting from a group of individuals who believe this to be the case,' and 'all the authentic programs have been properly notified and reported to Congress.' AARO also reports that executives, scientists and chief technology officers of the named companies met the Director of AARO and 'denied on the record that they have ever recovered, possessed, or engaged in reverse-engineering of extraterrestrial technology.' Those denials are artifacts with dates and signatories, and they are the most specific corporate denials on the record.

### What it has denied

AARO's 2024 historical review states that it 'found no empirical evidence for claims that the USG and private companies have been reverse-engineering extraterrestrial technology,' that claims 'involving specific people, known locations, technological tests, and documents' are 'inaccurate,' that the narrative is 'in large part, the result of circular reporting from a group of individuals who believe this to be the case,' and that 'all the authentic programs have been properly notified and reported to Congress through the congressional defense and/or intelligence committees.' Pentagon spokesperson Sue Gough said the Department had found no 'verifiable information to substantiate claims that any programs regarding the possession or reverse-engineering of extraterrestrial materials have existed in the past or exist currently.' These are dated institutional denials whose own supporting documents concede a DHS compartment proposed to protect 'non-human biologics' and a 2021 IC access-program expansion made 'without sufficient justification' to protect UAP reverse-engineering.

Roles the board assigns it: reverse-engineering, concealment, funding, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether any charter, directive or governing document for a legacy retrieval and reverse-engineering effort exists, and whether Mellon's 'governing documents' refers to documents he has seen or has been told about.
- The identity of the 2004 CIA think tank Puthoff says participated in a decision not to declassify, and whether any record of that deliberation exists.
- Which companies were named to AARO by interviewees and whose executives then denied on the record -- AARO does not name them.
- Whether the KONA BLUE PSAP proposal identified specific holders of the material it was designed to protect, and what the DHS Under Secretary was shown in 2011 to justify establishing it.
- Whether Nell's October 1, 2030 disclosure timeline was his own construct or reflected an actual programme milestone he had been briefed on.
- How AARO reconciles its finding that 'all the authentic programs have been properly notified' with its own admission that a 2021 IC Controlled Access Program was expanded 'without sufficient justification.'

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 82 of 1066, score 67). 10 connections, 7 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C2/A3/D3.

# 1970-1999: Post-Blue Book

## Lockheed Martin Corporation

Lockheed Martin Corporation is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1995; axes C3 A3 D0; 30 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Prime defense contractor; largest single recipient of DoD prime contract obligations.

### What it is

Lockheed Martin Corporation is the largest single recipient of United States defense contract obligations, formed by the 1995 merger of Lockheed and Martin Marietta. It operates cleared facilities accredited for Special Access Program work and runs the Advanced Development Programs organisation, the Skunk Works, which has built the U-2, SR-71 and F-117 among other classified aircraft. It is the only corporation named on the record, by a sworn witness in a congressional hearing, as a holder of recovered non-terrestrial material.

### Why it is implicated

Lockheed Martin sits at the intersection of the two conditions this board treats as necessary for a legacy programme to survive without congressional visibility: a company with the industrial capacity to store and work on recovered material, and a legal regime that can lift normal industrial security oversight off it. Under 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3) the Director of DCSA is relieved of National Industrial Security Program oversight for DoD special access programs whenever the Secretary or Deputy Secretary approves a carve-out provision. Under DoD Directive 5205.07, no contractor employee or consultant may provide SAP material to any member of Congress or their staff without the approval of the Director of the DoD SAP Central Office. Two sworn witnesses - George Knapp in September 2025 and Luis Elizondo in November 2024 - have testified to a specific, dated, blocked transaction in which material collected in the 1950s and held at a California facility was to move from a corporation to Bigelow's BAASS, and did not. Lockheed Martin has issued no public rebuttal of that sworn testimony.

### The longer account

The Lockheed Martin allegation is unusual on this board because it is the one that has been made under oath, in a hearing room, with a company name attached. On 9 September 2025, before the House Oversight Committee's Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets, George Knapp told the committee: 'It has been widely reported and suspected that Lockheed Martin is one of the contractors - the defense contractors, that has held this stuff, stored it away in secrecy, and tried to figure out how it works.' He then went further and put his own reporting on the record: 'I have confirmed on the record that Robert Bigelow and a trusted colleague from AAWSAP met with and negotiated with senior executives at Lockheed Martin and hammered out a deal wherein Bigelow's company, BAASS, would receive a quantity of unusual material that had been stashed away and protected at a facility in California. That material was not made here.' Later in the same hearing Rep. Tim Burchett asked which corporations hold the material. Knapp answered: 'Well, one of them is Lockheed.' He added a line that is, for the purposes of this map, more revealing than the accusation itself: 'I am not saying Lockheed's the bad guys. They are doing what they were asked to do. ... They have lied about this because that is what they are supposed to do.'

Ten months earlier, on 13 November 2024, Luis Elizondo had testified to the same transaction from inside the government side of it. Asked by Rep. Burlison whether the government or defence contractors possess technology, Elizondo said one reason his predecessor programme AAWSAP was trying to collect material of unknown origin was that 'there was an aerospace contract company that requested to divest itself of the material ... that was collected in the 1950s. Unfortunately, that did not actually occur.' Burlison then put the Christopher Sharp reporting to him directly - a transfer between Lockheed Martin, Bigelow Aerospace, and a CIA block - and Elizondo answered: 'What I can say is that it was blocked. Why it was blocked, I can only surmise. I was part of some conversations later on with some of those contract personnel where they had told all of us that is accurate.' He described the missing instrument: 'What we required was a memo from the Secretary of the Air Force in order to make that complete, and that never occurred.'

The official record does not name the company, but it does record the allegation. AARO's Historical Record Report, Volume I, logs an interviewee claim that an organisation 'was in possession of off-world material in 2009 and 2010,' that a separate interviewee 'participated in negotiations to return the material to the USG,' and that 'a former named senior CIA official quashed the proposal to remove the material from the corporation.' A separate entry logs a claim that an organisation held twelve spacecraft recovered from crash events prior to 1970, some allegedly intact, under a CIA partnership that ended in 1989. AARO says it found no empirical evidence for either. AARO's denial is procedurally notable in its own right: 'The executives, scientists, and chief technology officers of the companies named by interviewees met with the Director of AARO and denied on the record that they have ever recovered, possessed, or engaged in reverse-engineering of extraterrestrial technology.' AARO does not say which executives, at which companies, on what date, and describes no verification step beyond the meeting itself. Under this board's sourcing rule, that is an artefact of a statement, not evidence of the fact asserted.

What makes the allegation structurally survivable rather than merely unproven is the regulatory architecture around a company like Lockheed Martin. Industrial security oversight of cleared contractors is normally exercised by the Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency under the National Industrial Security Program. But 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3) provides that 'The Director, DCSA, will be relieved of this oversight function for DoD special access programs (SAPs) when the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense approves a carve-out provision in accordance with DoDD 5205.07.' A carve-out programme is therefore a cleared contractor facility from which the government's own standing industrial security inspector has been formally removed by name, at the direction of the second-ranking official in the Department.

The congressional side is narrowed by the same directive. DoD Directive 5205.07, reissued 12 September 2024, states at Section 3.1.g: 'No DoD contractor entity, contractor employee, contractor representative, or consultant will provide SAP material to any members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval.' That is a standing prior-restraint on contractor-to-Congress disclosure inside the SAP world, in an unclassified, publicly posted directive. The statutory reporting channel is narrower still: 10 U.S.C. 119(e) lets the Secretary of Defense waive the content of any SAP report to the defence committees on a case-by-case national security determination, in which case the information goes 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees' - twelve people, not six bodies.

So the record on Lockheed Martin has an unusual shape. Two witnesses under oath, one of them a former Pentagon official, describing the same blocked transfer with the same missing Secretary of the Air Force memorandum. An official report that catalogues the allegation, does not name the company, and reports an unverified denial from unnamed executives. A statutory and regulatory framework that would make exactly this kind of holding invisible to the ordinary inspection and reporting machinery. And, on the other side of the ledger, no contract, no facility designation, no shipping document, no inventory, no subpoena, no inspection, and no on-the-record statement from Lockheed Martin responding to sworn testimony that named it.

### What it has denied

Lockheed Martin has issued no public, on-the-record rebuttal of the sworn testimony of September 2025 naming it. The only denial in the documentary record is AARO's summary statement in its 2024 Historical Record Report that unnamed 'executives, scientists, and chief technology officers of the companies named by interviewees' met the Director of AARO and denied ever having recovered, possessed or reverse-engineered extraterrestrial technology.

Roles the board assigns it: custody, reverse-engineering, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether any Lockheed Martin facility has ever operated under a Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense-approved carve-out relieving DCSA of industrial security oversight, and if so for which programmes and over what period. Carve-out approvals are not published.
- The identity of the 'facility in California' described in Knapp's sworn testimony, and whether it is a company-owned site or leased space on a government-owned installation.
- Whether the Secretary of the Air Force memorandum Elizondo described as necessary and never issued exists in draft, and in whose files.
- Whether Lockheed Martin executives were among those AARO says met its Director, and on what date - AARO names neither the companies nor the individuals.
- Lockheed Martin's SEC filings report results attributable to classified programmes without identifying them; whether any of those programmes intersect the custody allegation cannot be tested from the public filings and is a line of research, not a claim.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 28 of 1066, score 99). 30 connections, 8 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 3 official denials recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D0.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(e) permits the Secretary of Defense to waive the inclusion of specified information in required SAP reports on a case-by-case national security determination, in which case the information and the waiver justification go only 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees.' Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/10/119
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3) provides that 'The Director, DCSA, will be relieved of this oversight function for DoD special access programs (SAPs) when the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense approves a carve-out provision in accordance with DoDD 5205.07' - the legal mechanism by which a cleared contractor facility can be removed from the government's standing industrial security inspection regime. Source: 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3), National Industrial Security Program Operating Manual (NISPOM) Rule, 85 FR 83312, 21 December 2020 - https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-32/subtitle-A/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-117/section-117.6

## United States Department of Energy

United States Department of Energy is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1977-10-01; axes C3 A3 D0; 20 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Successor to the Atomic Energy Commission; holder of Restricted Data authority independent of the executive classification system.

### What it is

The Department of Energy is the successor to the Atomic Energy Commission and the custodian of the United States nuclear weapons complex, including Los Alamos, Sandia, Lawrence Livermore and the Pantex assembly and disassembly plant, operated through the National Nuclear Security Administration. Under the Atomic Energy Act of 1954 it holds a government-wide classification authority, Restricted Data, that exists entirely outside the executive order classification system and is not subject to automatic declassification.

### Why it is implicated

DOE holds the one classification authority in the United States government that a legacy programme could hide behind indefinitely, because Restricted Data is created by statute rather than by executive order, applies government-wide, and never automatically declassifies. Its facilities are also the physical centre of gravity of the phenomenon's own documented history: the green fireball wave of 1948 to 1951 concentrated over Los Alamos and Sandia, Project GRUDGE recorded case clusters in the Los Alamos and Albuquerque area and at Oak Ridge, and Congress now requires AARO to report annually on incidents at nuclear weapons production, transportation and storage facilities. In the most recent reporting year the Administrator for Nuclear Security and the Nuclear Regulatory Commission Chairman sent AARO fifty incident reports, a 177.8 percent increase over the prior year, every one of which AARO records as not reported as UAP. A former DoD official named DOE under oath as one of the agencies with a role and no accountability. DOE has not appeared among the agencies transmitting records to the statutory National Archives collection.

### The longer account

The reason DOE belongs on a map of concealment has nothing to do with what anyone alleges and everything to do with statute. Restricted Data is defined by the Atomic Energy Act of 1954 and covers all data concerning the design, manufacture or utilisation of atomic weapons, the production of special nuclear material, and the use of special nuclear material in the production of energy. It is classified under the Act, not under an executive order. Everything else in the national security classification system flows from a presidential order, currently the 13526 lineage, and is therefore subject to automatic declassification at twenty-five years unless exempted. Restricted Data is not. It requires affirmative review before declassification, it cannot be declassified by an agency that did not originate it, and the authority is government-wide. DOE's implementing regulation is 10 CFR Part 1045, most recently overhauled by a final rule published in the Federal Register on 21 December 2018.

The practical consequence is that if a UAP incident occurred over a weapons production facility, a storage igloo, a transport convoy or a laboratory working on weapons physics, the record of it could be created as Restricted Data from the moment of its generation and would then be permanently outside the reach of the executive order declassification machinery that every UAP disclosure statute has assumed. The FY2024 NDAA's records collection provisions operate on federal records generally. They do not amend the Atomic Energy Act.

The history is dense. From late November 1948 through early 1951 a wave of green luminous objects was reported over New Mexico, concentrated near Los Alamos and Sandia and over other sensitive installations. On 16 February 1949 a conference at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory brought together laboratory director Norris Bradbury, Edward Teller, Frederick Reines, representatives of the FBI, the Atomic Energy Commission and the military, and Lincoln LaPaz of the University of New Mexico Institute of Meteoritics, who had personally observed the objects. LaPaz, after interviewing more than a hundred witnesses, advised the military and the AEC that the fireballs were most likely either unconventional defensive devices being tested by the United States or Soviet reconnaissance devices. The Air Force ran Project Twinkle from 1949 to 1951 to instrument the phenomenon, never fully implemented it, and concluded the objects were probably natural.

AARO's own Volume I preserves the pattern independently. It records Captain Edward Ruppelt of Project GRUDGE noting concentrations of cases in the Los Alamos and Albuquerque area, Oak Ridge, White Sands, Strategic Air Command locations, ports and industrial sites. It records that AARO interviewed five former Air Force members who served in and around intercontinental ballistic missile silos at Malmstrom, Ellsworth and elsewhere in connection with a narrative that UAP sightings near nuclear facilities resulted in malfunctioning missiles and a destroyed test reentry vehicle. It records that AARO continues to investigate these cases because of their potential impact on the readiness of the US nuclear programme. And it lists DOE among the agency archives AARO partnered with.

Congress has legislated the nuclear connection into permanent statutory reporting. Section 3373(k) of title 50 requires AARO's annual report to include the number and description of incidents of UAP associated with military nuclear assets, and separately the results of consultation with the Administrator for Nuclear Security regarding incidents at facilities or assets associated with the production, transportation or storage of nuclear weapons or their components, and with the Chairman of the Nuclear Regulatory Commission regarding nuclear power stations and fuel storage sites. That is a standing legal obligation on the nuclear weapons enterprise to account, annually, for anomalous objects over its facilities.

The FY2025 report is the most recent execution of it and the number is striking. AARO received fifty reports from the Administrator for Nuclear Security and the NRC Chairman regarding unmanned aerial system incidents near US nuclear infrastructure, weapons and launch sites, a 177.8 percent increase over the eighteen reported the previous year. Forty-seven of the fifty occurred between 1700 and 0500 local. Five persisted at least an hour, the longest about three hours. AARO then adds the sentence that carries all the weight: none of these incidents were reported as UAP. The nuclear complex is reporting a rising volume of unexplained aerial activity over the most sensitive sites in the country and categorising all of it, in advance, as drones.

The 2015 Pantex incident is the clearest recent case. A Department of Energy report describes security personnel at the nuclear weapons assembly and disassembly plant near Amarillo observing a diamond-shaped object in September 2015, hovering and moving in a northerly trajectory at roughly ten to fifteen miles per hour, described by witnesses as around four feet tall and two feet wide at the base and thinner at the top, with conflicting colour descriptions. The object was tracked by ground radar and photographed from a nearby radar tower, and the imagery was analysed by DOE's Sandia National Laboratories. The report was released in more complete form during the 2026 executive-branch disclosure tranches after earlier heavily redacted releases.

Luis Elizondo, testifying in November 2024, listed the Department of Energy among the White House, CIA, NASA and the Pentagon as agencies that play a role in the UAP issue while no one seems to be in charge, leading to unchecked power and corruption. As of the National Archives collection page's 22 May 2026 review, DOE had no transmittal listed in Record Group 615.

### What it has denied

DOE has issued no public denial of the legacy programme allegation and, unlike DoD, has not published a historical account of its involvement. Its posture is structural rather than rhetorical: it exercises a statutory classification authority that does not require it to deny anything, and its reporting to AARO categorises every recent incident near nuclear weapons infrastructure as an unmanned aerial system rather than as UAP.

Roles the board assigns it: custody, concealment, reverse-engineering.

### Open questions on this node

- DOE has not, on the public record, transmitted UAP records to the National Archives Record Group 615 under sections 1841-1843 of the FY2024 NDAA. Whether it has asserted that responsive material is Restricted Data and therefore outside the transfer obligation is undocumented and is the single highest-value question on this node.
- No released document establishes whether any UAP-related record has ever been classified as Restricted Data or Formerly Restricted Data. If one has, the FY2024 NDAA collection is structurally incomplete.
- AARO reports 50 incidents near nuclear weapons infrastructure from NNSA and NRC in a single year and states none were reported as UAP. The criteria by which the reporting agency classifies an unresolved object as an unmanned aerial system rather than a UAP have not been published.
- The Sandia National Laboratories imagery analysis of the 2015 Pantex object has not been released in full, and it is not known whether Sandia reached a conclusion on the object's identity.
- The complete record of the 16 February 1949 Los Alamos conference, the LaPaz interviews and the Project Twinkle instrument data has never been released as a set, and it is unclear which portions sit in DOE holdings versus Air Force holdings.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 49 of 1066, score 80). 21 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 2 official denials recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D0.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented, allegation shaped wording as stored.** AARO interviewed approximately 30 people 'who claimed to have insight into alleged USG involvement in off-world technology exploitation or to possess knowledge of UAP that have allegedly disrupted U.S. nuclear facilities in the past,' making the nuclear complex an explicit scope element of the government's own historical review. Source: AARO Historical Record Report Volume I, March 2024, Scope; https://media.defense.gov/2024/Mar/08/2003409233/-1/-1/0/DOPSR-CLEARED-508-COMPLIANT-HRRV1-08-MAR-2024-FINAL.PDF
- **Tier 1 documented.** AARO lists the Department of Energy among the agency archives it partnered with for the historical review, alongside DIA, CIA, NSA, NGA, the Services and the Combatant Commands. ## EarthTech International / Institute for Advanced Studies at Austin

EarthTech International / Institute for Advanced Studies at Austin is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1985; axes C3 A2 D3; 14 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Private research firm, Austin, TX; largest single DIRD producer.

### What it is

EarthTech International, Inc. is a private research company in Austin, Texas, incorporated in 1991 as the corporate vehicle for the Institute for Advanced Studies at Austin, which Harold E. Puthoff founded in 1985. Its declared research program covers spacetime and gravitation theory, the quantum vacuum and zero-point energy, modifications to standard electrodynamics, interstellar flight science, and SETI, framed as inputs to innovative space propulsion and energy generation. Puthoff is president, CEO and board chairman; Eric W. Davis is chief science officer.

### Why it is implicated

EarthTech is the intellectual clearinghouse where the legacy program's technical vocabulary was written down and then sold back to the government as reference literature. Under the Defense Intelligence Agency's Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program, EarthTech was subcontracted through Robert Bigelow's BAASS, and Puthoff and Davis commissioned the set of expert studies that DIA published as Defense Intelligence Reference Documents on traversable wormholes, antigravity, metamaterials and invisibility cloaking. That output supplied the exact conceptual frame, non-chemical propulsion, spacetime metric engineering, exotic materials, that Congress later wrote into statute as the boundary of a covered reverse-engineering program. AARO's Historical Record Report Volume I aims squarely at this network without naming it, attributing the reverse-engineering allegations to a group involved in UAP endeavors "since at least 2009" and tied to "a cancelled DIA program and the subsequent but failed attempt to reestablish this program under DHS, called KONA BLUE." AARO's own account therefore places these people at the center of both the DIA program and its DHS successor attempt, while insisting nothing was ever recovered.

### The longer account

Harold E. Puthoff earned his PhD at Stanford in 1967 as a theoretical and experimental physicist working on lasers, and spent the 1970s running the SRI International parapsychology program with Russell Targ under intelligence-community sponsorship, the lineage that became Project STARGATE. In 1985 he founded the Institute for Advanced Studies at Austin; in 1991 it was incorporated under EarthTech International, Inc. Both remain co-located in Austin. Eric W. Davis, whose declared specializations include breakthrough propulsion physics, beamed energy propulsion, directed energy weapons, general relativity, quantum field theory, quantum gravity and what he calls SETI-xenoarchaeology, is chief science officer of both entities.

The institutional significance is not the physics. It is that EarthTech occupied, for four decades, a specific structural position: a small, privately held, security-cleared research house with no shareholders to answer to, no publication obligations, and a standing relationship with the parts of the defense and intelligence enterprise that buy speculative propulsion analysis. That is precisely the shape of entity a legacy program would need, small enough to compartment, technical enough to be credible, private enough to sit outside the FOIA perimeter.

The documented engagement is AAWSAP. In 2008 the Defense Intelligence Agency awarded a contract to Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies, reported at up to $22 million over five years, to assess advanced aerospace threats. Puthoff served as the program's chief scientist on the BAASS side, and EarthTech was subcontracted to produce the written product. Puthoff and Davis commissioned a series of thirty-eight expert studies, which DIA then issued as Defense Intelligence Reference Documents. The subjects released into the public record include traversable wormholes and negative energy, antigravity, metamaterials for aerospace applications, and invisibility cloaking. Whatever one makes of the science, the procurement fact is unambiguous: a U.S. intelligence agency paid, through a contractor and a subcontractor, for a reference library on how one would build and analyze a craft that violates the propulsion assumptions of every acquisition program in the department. Fifteen years later Congress drafted the covered-activity definition for UAP reverse-engineering as any aerospace craft using "propulsion technology other than chemical propellants, solar power, and electric ion thrust", the DIRD library's subject matter, rendered as statutory text.

Davis is also the pivot of the two most consequential unofficial documents in the modern record. In October 2002 he met with retired Vice Admiral Thomas Ray Wilson, former director of DIA; the handwritten notes of that conversation, describing a special access program under contractor control that had denied access to a senior flag officer, surfaced publicly in 2019 as the Wilson-Davis memo. In July 2020 the New York Times reported that Davis, then a contractor for Aerospace Corporation, had briefed a Defense Department agency in October 2019 and Senate Armed Services Committee staff about retrievals from "off-world vehicles not made on this earth." Both items are contested; neither is invented. Both were produced by the chief science officer of EarthTech International.

The AARO response is where this entity becomes central rather than peripheral. Volume I never names EarthTech, Puthoff, Davis or Bigelow. It does not need to. It states that the modern reverse-engineering claims stem "largely" from "a consistent group of individuals who have been involved in various UAP-related endeavors since at least 2009," and that many of them were "involved in or supportive of a cancelled DIA program and the subsequent but failed attempt to reestablish this program under DHS, called KONA BLUE." The cancelled DIA program is AAWSAP. AARO's rebuttal is therefore built on the same population that AARO concedes staffed a real, funded DIA program and then attempted, unsuccessfully, to stand up a DHS prospective special access program to continue it. AARO also reports resolving a claimed anomalous material sample to magnesium "and bismuth with some other trace elements, such as lead" by materials characterization, the layered magnesium-bismuth specimen that has circulated through this network for decades and been analyzed by parties associated with it.

The network extends forward. Davis appears on the team roster of the Disclosure Foundation, the organization chaired by former Deputy Assistant Secretary of Defense for Intelligence Christopher Mellon, whose published recommendations two of the three FY2026 NDAA UAP provisions mirror. So the same technical population that wrote the DIA reference library, that AARO identifies as the source of the circular reporting, and that pitched KONA BLUE to DHS, is also inside the advocacy apparatus that now shapes what Congress writes into the defense authorization act. That is not a contradiction. It is what a closed technical community looks like when the subject matter has no other constituency.

### What it has denied

EarthTech does not publish denials; it publishes physics. Its public posture is that of an ordinary small research institute working on quantum vacuum energy and advanced propulsion, with no reference on its own site to crash retrieval, custody of material, or the programs its principals are alleged to have served. That silence is itself a posture: the entity most persistently named in reverse-engineering allegations has never issued a dated, attributable statement addressing them, which leaves the government's characterizations, AARO's unnamed 'consistent group of individuals', as the only on-the-record account.

Roles the board assigns it: reverse-engineering, research, custody, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The DIA contract number and full obligation history for AAWSAP, and the subcontract instrument and dollar value flowing from BAASS to EarthTech International.
- The complete list of the 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents, their individual authors, and which remain unreleased.
- Whether EarthTech International has held prime contracts, grants or CRADAs with DoD, DOE, DIA, DARPA or the national laboratories independent of the BAASS subcontract.
- Whether EarthTech has at any time had physical custody of claimed anomalous material, under what chain of custody, and whether any of it was government-furnished.
- The provenance and current custodian of the layered magnesium-bismuth specimen AARO says it characterized.
- Whether Puthoff or Davis ever received the AARO notification the FY2024 IAA provision would have required, or would have been covered by it.
- The authenticated status of the Wilson-Davis memo: Wilson has publicly disputed it, and no independent authentication of the notes has been established.
- Puthoff's formal role and title at To The Stars Academy, and the corporate relationship, if any, between TTSA and EarthTech.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 80 of 1066, score 68). 14 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C3/A2/D3.

## Alien Reproduction Vehicle (ARV) / Flux Liner

Alien Reproduction Vehicle (ARV) / Flux Liner is a program on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1988-11-12; axes C0 A0 D3; 7 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Claimed saucer-shaped craft said to have been exhibited at Norton AFB on 12 November 1988; no record of its existence has been opened.

### What it is

A claimed saucer-shaped craft, called the Alien Reproduction Vehicle and the Flux Liner, said to have been shown in a hangar at Norton Air Force Base on 12 November 1988. No record of its existence has been opened. The whole account descends from one second-hand relay: an industrial designer said he saw three such craft, and an aerospace illustrator who was not present drew them from that verbal description. Two primary documents carry the description, William B. Scott's unpublished 1990 interview notes and the Disclosure Project's April 2001 Briefing Document, and they are why this node exists, not evidence that the craft does.

### Why it is implicated

It is on this map as the worked example of a rule the board adopted on 18 September 2026: the thing claimed about is a node in its own right, and it is the hub every claimant runs into. Ten people arguing about a place, joined only to each other, hide the convergence; joined to the place, they show it. This is the first craft node the board has ever carried, and its edges are deliberately mixed: the sole eyewitness, the illustrator who drew it, the trade-press editor who investigated and never published, the anonymous site that sells reconstructions of it, and a government scientist who says it never existed. Its degree means a lot is claimed here. It does not mean any of it is true, and the tier on each edge says which is which.

### The longer account

The argument for a node with no evidence behind it is that the alternative is worse. Before this build the board carried Steven Greer, the Disclosure Project, the Brown patents and the contractor estate, and carried nothing for the object all of them are talking about. A reader could not see that the ARV is a single claim with many carriers, because the carriers were joined to each other and to institutions, never to the thing itself. Building the hub makes the shape legible and, more usefully, makes the shape falsifiable: every edge into it is dated, typed and tiered, so the reader can count how much of the convergence is documented. The answer here is: very little. The strongest artefact is a working file an Aviation Week editor never published, and inside it the witness refuses to stand behind his own figures and disowns part of the drawing made from his account. The sizes then grow more precise with each retelling, which is the opposite of what happens to a measurement. The claimed lift mechanism is attributed to a Thomas Townsend Brown patent, and the board's own record is that neither Brown patent claims anti-gravity: both claim thrust by electrical fields in a dielectric medium. That join is the reason this thread belongs to a board about defense contractors at all, and it is also the reason it does not support what it is usually cited to support.

Roles the board assigns it: reverse-engineering, disclosure, research.

### Open questions on this node

- The Aviation Week and Space Technology issue of 1 October 1990, which the 1990 notes name as carrying McCandlish's two paintings. It is the only element of this thread published in the contemporary trade press and nobody has opened it.
- The McCandlish cut-away drawing itself, as printed in the Disclosure Project Briefing Document's plate section, which this session did not extract.
- Whether any record of Brad Sorensen exists under that surname. The 1990 document gives a first name, a school, a client list and an hourly rate, and nobody in the opened record appears to have tried.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 299 of 1066, score 37). 7 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A0/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** THE SIZES HAVE DRIFTED, AND THE DRIFT RUNS ONE WAY, across three opened documents. The 1990 interview notes give 'The small one was like 20 ft, the next 60 ft. diameter and the next one,...maybe 120 ft. diam. Just a guess; don't hold me to that.' The April 2001 Briefing Document gives the smallest as 'about 24 feet in diameter at its widest part, right at the base', the next as '60 feet in diameter at the base', and 'the largest of these vehicles was about 120 to 130 feet in diameter'. The arvp.co.uk FLUX 1988 page, read 18 September 2026, gives 'one with a 130-foot diameter, another with a 60-foot diameter, and a smaller one measuring 24 feet in diameter', with no disclaimer. Each retelling is more precise than the last about a figure the witness explicitly refused to stand behind. Source: Scott interview notes transcript p 10, https://www.arvp.co.uk/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/SORENSEN-1990-INTERVIEW.pdf, 1990-12-03; Disclosure Project Briefing Document section 3.8.5, https://archive.org/stream/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument_djvu.txt, 2000-12; arvp.co.uk FLUX 1988 page, https://www.arvp.co.uk/flux-01/, read 2026-09-18
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE WITNESS DISOWNS PART OF THE DRAWING. Shown McCandlish's sketches in December 1990, Sorensen says of the manned vehicle: 'this is probably the farthest from what I saw, and no, I didn't see anything with an intake up above the windows like this', and 'mark extrapolates a bit too much'. Of the saucer drawing he says the opposite: 'you've got a drawing, as accurate as I could get [the round, saucer-looking thing]. This dwg is so GD..accurate that I can't be much more specific than this.' Source: William B. Scott, interview notes on 'Brad', https://www.arvp.co.uk/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/SORENSEN-1990-INTERVIEW.pdf, transcript p 10, 1990-12-03
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** On the capacitor stack, in the illustrator's own words in the 2001 Briefing Document: around the middle of the vehicle 'a large plastic casting that had this big set of copper coils in it', 'about 18 inches wide at the top, and about 8 to 9 inches thick', with 'maybe 15 to 20 stacked layers of copper coils inside of it'. The bottom disc was 'about 11 or 12 inches thick', containing 'exactly 48 sections like thin slices of pizza pie', each 'full of half-inch-thick copper plates, and each of the 48 sections had 8 copper plates', alternating negative and positive four times. Source: Disclosure Project Briefing Document, section 3.8.5, https://archive.org/stream/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument_djvu.txt, 2000-12
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** On the claimed lift mechanism and its electrical architecture: 'what I was looking at was a huge Tesla coil, which is kind of like an open-air transformer', running off 'two large 24-volt marine-style batteries', stepping up 'through the secondary coil, which is on the column in the middle', with the ability to 'selectively put the voltage on any of these 48 capacitor sections', at 'probably something between, say, half a million and a million volts of electricity'. Source: Disclosure Project Briefing Document, section 3.8.5, https://archive.org/stream/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument_djvu.txt, 2000-12
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** On the crew compartment and the central column: the visible dome 'was actually the upper half of a big sphere-shaped crew compartment that was in the middle of the vehicle'; 'On the inside of the crew compartment was a big column that ran down through the middle, and there were four ejection seats mounted back-to-back on the upper half of this column. Then, right in the middle of the column, was a large flywheel of some kind.' Source: Disclosure Project Briefing Document, section 3.8.5, https://archive.org/stream/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument_djvu.txt, 2000-12

Source: Disclosure Project Briefing Document, section 3.8.5, https://archive.org/stream/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument_djvu.txt, 2000-12

Shown McCandlish's sketches in December 1990, Sorensen says of the manned vehicle: 'this is probably the farthest from what I saw, and no, I didn't see anything with an intake up above the windows like this', and 'mark extrapolates a bit too much'. - **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The 1990 primary document gives the three craft their nursery-rhyme names and the witness's own guessed sizes: 'This was a really interesting craft--there were three of them. When I walked into the room....well, I thought of something stupid--like a nursery rhyme: daddy bear, mommy bear, baby bear. Like they'd been put in a Xerox machine--the same proportions, just diff. sizes. The small one was like 20 ft, the next 60 ft. diameter and the next one,...maybe 120 ft. diam. Just a guess; don't hold me to that.' Source: William B. Scott, interview notes on 'Brad', https://www.arvp.co.uk/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/SORENSEN-1990-INTERVIEW.pdf, transcript p 10, 1990-12-03
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The April 2001 Briefing Document is the origin in print of the craft's two names, in the illustrator's own words: 'Well, this craft was what they called the Alien Reproduction Vehicle; it was also nicknamed the Flux Liner.' Source: Disclosure Project Briefing Document, section 3.8.5, https://archive.org/stream/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument_djvu.txt, 2000-12
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The account attributes the claimed lift effect to Thomas Townsend Brown. The 2001 testimony describes an experiment by 'Mark Stambough' that floated a plate capacitor at about a million volts 'in accordance with principles that had been described in a patent that was filed back in the late 1950s/early 1960s by a gentleman called Thomas Townsend Brown. Brown and another individual by the name of Dr. Biefield had done this, so this effect became known as the Biefield-Brown effect.' The direction of motion is given as toward the positively charged plate. The board's own record of both candidate patents is that NEITHER claims anti-gravity: US 3,187,206, filed 9 May 1958, opens 'This invention relates to an electrical device for producing thrust by the direct operation of electrical fields', and US 2,949,550, filed 3 July 1957, claims 'Thrust producing apparatus' operating by impressing high voltage between a body and an insulated electrode 'whereby to cause relative movement of an ionizable dielectric medium'. It is the ACCOUNT that reinterprets electrokinetic thrust as levitation. Source: Disclosure Project Briefing Document, section 3.8.5, https://archive.org/stream/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument_djvu.txt, 2000-12, read against the board's existing claims on thomas-townsend-brown for US 3,187,206 and US 2,949,550
- **Tier 2 named person on the record, allegation shaped wording as stored.** The case against, from inside the classification line. On the Jesse Michels episode published 12 December 2024, video id LnAiNChnuEQ, Eric Davis said that no ARV exists and none has ever been built, that the Northrop Grumman and Area 51 ARV rumours are 'all myth', that they originate with Bob Lazar-type storytellers who 'believe their own bullshit' rather than with deliberate disinformation, and that the CIA 'shut it all down' in 1989. The episode is identified here by video id and not by title: research/leads/michels-corpus-03.md file 04 recorded it as 'CIA Scientist: George Bush Was Briefed on UFOs (ft. Eric Davis)' on the channel 'American Alchemy', and as opened on 18 September 2026 the same video id carries the title 'Eric Davis: A US President Was Briefed on UFOs!' on the channel 'Jesse Michels'. The corpus timestamp 0:36:24 to 0:38:00 falls inside the video's 52:45 runtime. - Alien Reproduction Vehicle (ARV) / Flux Liner comes in from **Thomas Townsend Brown** (person, board tier A), a research link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: The account's claimed lift lineage, which the patents themselves do not support. THE EDGE MUST NOT BE READ AS ANTI-GRAVITY. The board's own record is that NEITHER Brown patent claims anti-gravity: US 3,187,206, filed 9 May 1958, and US 2,949,550, filed 3 July 1957, both claim thrust by electrical fields in a dielectric medium. It is the ACCOUNT that reinterprets electrokinetic thrust as levitation. McCandlish attributes the claimed effect to 'a patent that was filed back in the late 1950s/early 1960s by a gentleman called Thomas Townsend Brown', which on the board's own record is one of those two. Sources: Disclosure Project Briefing Document, section 3.8.5, https://archive.org/stream/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument/DisclosureProjectBriefingDocument_djvu.txt, dated 2001-04.
- Alien Reproduction Vehicle (ARV) / Flux Liner comes in from **'Brad' Sorensen** (person, board tier B), a witness-chain link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: Sole claimed eyewitness to the three craft. Every substantive claim about the craft traces to him and to nobody else. His own 1990 words limit it: the sizes are 'Just a guess; don't hold me to that', and of the manned vehicle in McCandlish's drawing, 'this is probably the farthest from what I saw'. Sources: William B. Scott, interview notes on 'Brad', 3 December 1990, scanned PDF hosted by arvp.co.uk, https://www.arvp.co.uk/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/SORENSEN-1990-INTERVIEW.pdf, dated 1990-12-11.
- Alien Reproduction Vehicle (ARV) / Flux Liner comes in from **Zero Point: The Story of Mark McCandlish and the Flux Liner** (document, board tier C), a investigative link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: A feature documentary carrying the claim to a later audience. A later carrier rather than a source. The film itself was not opened; the node rests on the Vimeo channel, the arvp.co.uk link and the user-maintained IMDb listing. Written as 'investigative' because 'media' is not one of this board's edge types. # 2000-2016: AAWSAP / AATIP

## Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program (AAWSAP)

Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program (AAWSAP) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2007 to 2012; axes C3 A3 D3; 59 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: dia-program.

### What it is

The Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program was a Defense Intelligence Agency effort solicited publicly on 18 August 2008 as HHM402-08-R-0211 by DIA's Virginia Contracting Activity and awarded on 22 September 2008 as firm-fixed-price contract HHM402-08-C-0072 to Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies, LLC of Las Vegas. Its stated objective was to understand the physics and engineering of breakthrough aerospace weapon system applications out to the year 2050 across twelve technical areas. It ran to 2012 and produced thirty-eight Defense Intelligence Reference Documents plus a much larger body of work that has never been released.

### Why it is implicated

AAWSAP is the only documented instance in the modern record of the United States government funding a private company to study the physics of observed anomalous flight performance, and its own procurement file shows the seams through which such work escapes normal visibility. The solicitation was posted as a total small business set-aside under a classification code meaning 'Special studies and analysis - not R&D,' place of performance was the contractor's own facility, and the Top Secret facility clearance requirement was formally amended so that it was not required prior to contract implementation. The statement of work provides that any accountable property the contractor acquires with government funds becomes government-owned property and must be inventoried and returned within sixty days of contract end - a custody provision whose closeout has never been publicly documented. Senator Harry Reid asked the Deputy Secretary of Defense to convert the effort into a special access program on 24 June 2009 and was refused. The unreleased output of this contract, which its own government manager has described as running to more than a hundred detailed reports, is the largest identified block of missing documentary material in the modern record.

### The longer account

AAWSAP is the best-documented UAP effort in the history of the United States government, and the reason is mundane: it was a competed commercial-items procurement, so it left a paper trail that the Freedom of Information Act could reach.

The solicitation went up on FedBizOpps as a combined synopsis/solicitation on 18 August 2008 under number HHM402-08-R-0211, issued by DIA's Virginia Contracting Activity at Bolling AFB, with responses due 5 September 2008. It was coded B, 'Special studies and analysis - not R&D,' under NAICS 541330, Engineering Services, and it was 'solicited as a total small business set aside' with a size standard of twenty-five million dollars. The Statement of Objectives, dated 18 July 2008, is unambiguous about what was wanted: 'The objective of this program is to understand the physics and engineering of these applications as they apply to the foreign threat out to the far term, i.e., from now through the year 2050. Primary focus is on breakthrough technologies and applications that create discontinuities in currently evolving technology trends. The focus is not on extrapolations of current aerospace technology.' Twelve technical areas were named, covering lift, propulsion, control, power generation, spatial and temporal translation, materials, configuration and structure, signature reduction, human interface, human effects, armament including directed energy, and other peripheral areas.

The qualification language is the tell. Offerors were asked to 'provide examples, conducted within the past 7 years, of having performed, as a prime contractor, the analysis, design, construction, and flight testing of high performance aircraft and/or satellites,' with the note that 'Success in this program is dependent upon this capability and experience.' The government was not shopping for a literature review.

The award document, Standard Form 1449, records contract HHM402-08-C-0072, awarded 22 September 2008 to BIGELOW AEROSPACE ADVANCED SPACE STUDIES, LLC, 4640 S Eastern Ave, Las Vegas NV, CAGE 54JU7, DUNS 827037768, with a total award amount of $21,948,810.00 and a base year of 22 September 2008 to 29 September 2009 followed by option years. The solicitation had anticipated 'a base year of 12 months from date of award plus four (4) 12 month option years' running to 30 September 2013. Deliverable line items were status reports, a project management plan for tasks 3.a.1 through 3.a.12, research reports for the same tasks, a Comprehensive Integrated Threat Assessment, travel and other direct costs.

Three provisions in the statement of work matter more than the money. First, security: 'The contractor shall comply with the terms and conditions of the DD-254 BEFORE initiating any classified effort,' and 'Contractor shall provide a work facility (including unclassified information systems) with a Top Secret Facility Clearance granted by the Defense Security Service (DSS).' But an amendment posted with the solicitation changed DD Form 254 Block 13 from 'THE CONTRACTOR WILL BE REQUIRED TO HAVE A TOP SECRET FACILITY CLEARANCE GRANTED BY THE DEFENSE SECURITY SERVICE (DSS) PRIOR CONTRACT IMPLEMENTATION' to 'THE CONTRACTOR WILL NOT BE REQUIRED TO HAVE A TOP SECRET FACILITY CLEARANCE ... PRIOR TO CONTRACT IMPLEMENTATION. HOWEVER, IT MUST BE OBTAINED AS SOON AS POSSIBLE DURING THE INITIAL YEAR OF THE CONTRACT.' The facility-clearance bar was lowered before award.

Second, place of performance: 'The work under this contract shall be performed at the Contractor's facility.' Third, and most consequential for this board, property: 'Any accountable property acquired by the contractor with government funds during the execution of this contract shall become government owned property. ... This property must be inventoried and returned to the government not more than 60 days after the end of the contract.' There is, in the released file, no closeout inventory.

What the contractor actually did is not seriously in dispute, and AARO concedes it. AARO's Historical Record Report states: 'Although investigating UFO/UAP was not specifically outlined in the contract's statement of work, the selected private sector organization conducted UFO research with the support of the DIA program manager. This research included: reviewing new cases and much older Project BLUE BOOK cases, operating debriefing and investigatory teams, and proposals to set up laboratories to examine any recovered UFO materials.' AARO adds that 'DIA did not seek, nor specifically authorize, this work though a DIA employee set up and managed the contract with the private sector organization,' and that the effort also investigated an alleged hotspot of UAP and paranormal activity at a Utah property then owned by the head of the contractor.

The special access question is the hinge. AARO records that 'On 24 June 2009, Senator Reid sent a letter to then Deputy Secretary of Defense William Lynn III requesting that AAWSAP/AATIP be made a DoD Special Access Program. Deputy Secretary Lynn declined to do so based on the recommendation of then-Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence, James R. Clapper, Jr., that such a designation was not justified.' That refusal is documented in the FOIA record alongside DIA talking papers prepared for Reid's visits to the Director of DIA in January and May 2009 and an eighteen-page packet presented to the Deputy Secretary in November 2009.

AARO's account and the contractor's account of what the programme produced differ by an order of magnitude. AARO says the contract 'produced exploratory papers addressing the 12 scientific areas tasked in the contract's statement of work' which 'were never thoroughly peer reviewed,' and that it 'has yet to uncover any other substantive UAP case work.' The releasable record contains thirty-seven of thirty-eight Defense Intelligence Reference Documents. What is not in the record is the 494-page BAASS Ten Month Report, the Utah ranch investigation report, the personnel roster, obligation-by-year funding data, and the balance of the material that the government programme manager has described as running past a hundred reports. Under this board's sourcing rule, AARO's characterisation is a Tier 2 record of what AARO said, not a Tier 1 record of what exists.

### What it has denied

AARO's 2024 Historical Record Report concedes the UFO research but characterises AAWSAP as an unauthorised drift from an authorised aerospace threat study, states that DIA 'did not seek, nor specifically authorize' the UFO work, that the deliverables were 'exploratory papers' that were 'never thoroughly peer reviewed,' and that AARO 'has yet to uncover any other substantive UAP case work conducted by AAWSAP/AATIP.'

Roles the board assigns it: funding, reverse-engineering, custody, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the property inventory and return required within sixty days of contract end was ever performed, what was on it, and where that property is now. The statement of work made everything the contractor bought with government money government property.
- The obligated funding by year. The Standard Form 1449 records a total award amount of $21,948,810.00, but the line-item prices are redacted under FOIA exemption (b)(4) and no year-by-year obligation data has been released.
- The full deliverable inventory. Thirty-seven of thirty-eight DIRDs are public; the Ten Month Report, the Comprehensive Integrated Threat Assessment, and the monthly status reports are not.
- Whether the Top Secret facility clearance the amended DD-254 required 'as soon as possible during the initial year of the contract' was ever granted, and on what date.
- Who the DIA program manager's chain of command was, and what internal record exists of the decision not to stop the UFO work that AARO says DIA never authorised.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 2 of 1066, score 174). 61 connections, 53 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D3.

DIA Talking Paper U-973-09 records that the RFP broke the work into twelve technical areas - lift, propulsion, control, armament, signature reduction and the rest - and the Defense Intelligence Reference Documents are the products those areas were delivered as. Roughly $22 million was placed in the classified defense appropriation at the direction of Senate leadership and spent through DIA solicitation HHM402-08-R-0211 and the resulting prime contract.
