## Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies (BAASS), LLC

Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies (BAASS), LLC is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2007 to 2012; axes C3 A2 D2; 19 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: contractor.

### What it is

Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies (BAASS) was the Las Vegas company, an arm of Robert Bigelow's aerospace operation, that held the Defense Intelligence Agency's Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program (AAWSAP) contract beginning in 2008. It staffed physicists, intelligence veterans and field investigators, produced the 38 technical papers that DIA later designated Defense Intelligence Reference Documents, and ran field investigations at Skinwalker Ranch and elsewhere. Funding is generally reported at $22 million across 2007-2012, with the DIA declining to renew in 2010.

### Why it is implicated

BAASS is the single best-documented instance of the US government routing intelligence money to a private aerospace contractor to study UAP and 'breakthrough technology applications employed in future aerospace weapon systems.' It is the exact structural shape the legacy-program hypothesis predicts: an intelligence agency sponsor, a cleared contractor with its own facilities in Nevada, cover stories for field investigators, and an explicit internal instruction that the sponsor's identity 'should NEVER be released or discussed in public.' Its deliverables are the government's own reverse-engineering reading list, warp metrics, traversable wormholes, metamaterials, invisibility cloaking. It also acquired custody of civilian-collected physical evidence by buying MUFON's case database, moving material from a public non-profit into a DIA-funded contractor without the reporting witnesses knowing who the ultimate sponsor was. Finally, the solicitation was written, by the sponsoring senator's own account, specifically to avoid the words UFO or UAP.

### The longer account

The BAASS story begins with money and a Senate Majority Leader. In 2008 the Defense Intelligence Agency issued a solicitation for the Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program, whose stated purpose was to explore 'potential breakthrough technology applications employed in future aerospace weapon systems.' Harry Reid of Nevada has said publicly that he was behind the funding, and has explained the wording directly: 'The people putting out the bid thought it would be better that it didn't say flying saucers or unidentified flying objects. It was thought by many that it would just draw too much attention, and by it being phrased the way it was, we had somebody from the Defense Intelligence Agency draw out the specs of it. It covered that anyway.' Reid also stated flatly that Bigelow 'was not pre-selected' and that the award followed a competitive bid. The record shows the solicitation produced a single bidder, and the winner was a Nevada constituent of the senator who arranged the money. Steven Aftergood, writing for the Federation of American Scientists in January 2019, put it in a sentence: the program was 'apparently initiated at the behest of then-Senate Majority Leader Harry Reid, with most of the funding directed to a Nevada constituent of his.'

What BAASS actually did splits into three streams, and the split matters for the board. The first is the technical literature. BAASS assembled a network of outside scientists, most prominently Hal Puthoff and Eric Davis, who produced 38 papers that DIA designated Defense Intelligence Reference Documents. DIA sent the title list to Senate Armed Services chairman John McCain and ranking member Jack Reed in January 2018, marked For Official Use Only, and released it publicly under FOIA on 16 January 2019. The titles include 'Traversable Wormholes, Stargates, and Negative Energy' and papers on metamaterials, programmable matter, high-power microwave technology, metallic glasses and biological effects. Aftergood's summary is worth quoting because it is the government's own science-integrity problem stated plainly: many of the titles are 'highly conjectural and well beyond the boundaries of current science, engineering, or military intelligence.' For the board's purposes, the DIRD list is not evidence that any of this works. It is evidence of what an intelligence agency thought it needed to understand, and therefore of what it believed it might be looking at.

The second stream is field investigation. BAASS sent investigators to Utah's Uinta Basin and to Skinwalker Ranch, 512 acres owned by Bigelow personally until he sold it to Brandon Fugal in 2016. Internal BAASS documents obtained by The Debrief instructed investigators to pose as researchers from the local oil fields and never to reveal the names of 'BAASS, Robert Bigelow, Bigelow Aerospace…and most certainly [the] sponsors' identity should NEVER be released or discussed in public.' The ultimate sponsor was the DIA. The same documents record interviews about a bipedal 'dog-like creature' over six feet tall smelling of sulfur, 'little people' three to four feet tall, and a ghostly figure on a mesa. A progress report obtained separately by Tim McMillan indicated BAASS was attempting to establish communication with a non-human entity it believed occupied the ranch. A former team member told The Debrief that the actual job was to collect anything anomalous and work out 'how it could lead to new technology,' and that data quality was compromised because some ranch guards embellished incidents in hopes of a bonus.

The third stream, and the most under-analysed, is data and evidence acquisition. In 2009 BAASS funded the Mutual UFO Network and bought its entire reporting database, witness reports, witness identifying information, and collected evidence. James Carrion, MUFON's international director at the time, told The Debrief that all 'physical evidence that needed to be analyzed went to BAASS,' that he does not know whether any of it was returned, and that he himself did not know DIA was the sponsor when MUFON entered the agreement. Members of the public filing reports agreed only that their information might go to an unnamed 'third party.' BAASS also sought data from France's GEIPAN, approached Canadian researcher Chris Rutkowski for Canadian government cases, and sent a team to Brazil in 2009 to collect evidence from a recent event. That is a private contractor, funded by a US intelligence agency, systematically vacuuming up the international civilian UAP evidentiary base under a cover identity.

DIA declined to renew in 2010 and the investigative team was disbanded; a former employee described being told by phone that they no longer had a job. Some analysts held Secret clearances and were reportedly in the process of upgrading to Top Secret when terminated. What happened next is the pivot into AATIP under Luis Elizondo, and thence into the 2017 New York Times story. Elizondo has said he was not involved with BAASS but knew the individuals, and that they 'decided to cast a very wide net.' Michael Gold, testifying to House Oversight on 13 November 2024, gave AAWSAP a retrospective defence that is also a concession about how thin the official record is: 'If not for the work of the Advanced Aerospace Weapon Systems Applications Program (AAWSAP), Commander Dietrich's experience may have been lost to history.'

What the record does not establish: it does not show BAASS handling recovered non-human material, and no DIRD is a reverse-engineering report on a specific artefact. It does not resolve whether AAWSAP and AATIP were the same program under two names, the DoD has given conflicting accounts. And it does not account for what became of the MUFON physical evidence.

### What it has denied

The Pentagon told The Debrief that DIA was not investigating UFOs, a position The Debrief noted is contradicted by the documentary record; the confusion is traceable to a solicitation that, by Harry Reid's own account, was deliberately written to omit the words UFO and UAP. Reid separately denied that Bigelow was pre-selected for the contract.

Roles the board assigns it: research, reverse-engineering, funding, custody, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Was $22 million the contract ceiling, the obligated amount, or the expended amount, and how much of the field-investigation work was funded from Bigelow's personal wealth rather than appropriated money?
- What happened to the physical evidence transferred from MUFON to BAASS, and does any of it remain in private custody?
- Did BAASS retain copies of the 38 DIRDs and the collected international case data after the contract lapsed in 2010, and were those holdings ever inventoried for the NARA UAP Records Collection?
- Who else was on the AAWSAP distribution list, and which offices outside DIA received the DIRDs?
- Are AAWSAP and AATIP the same program under two names, sequential programs, or a program and a successor counterintelligence portfolio? DoD has given inconsistent accounts.
- Where did BAASS-cleared analysts go after the late-2010 terminations, and did any move to successor programs or to contractors named elsewhere on this board?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 15 of 1066, score 113). 21 connections, 11 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C3/A2/D2.

## Advanced Aerospace Threat Identification Program (AATIP / AAITP)

Advanced Aerospace Threat Identification Program (AATIP / AAITP) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2009 to 2012; axes C3 A3 D3; 14 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: dia-program.

### What it is

AATIP is the unclassified nickname attached to a Defense Intelligence Agency effort that ran on congressional earmarks from FY2008 through roughly 2012, executed under contract by Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies LLC (BAASS) of North Las Vegas. Primary documents show the name used in at least three incompatible ways: as the DIA contract line (Advanced Aerospace Threat and Identification Program), as the internal DIA program office designation (AAWSAP, the Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program), and as a later OUSD(I) activity that Luis Elizondo says he ran and that DoD public affairs says never officially existed. The naming collision is not trivia; it is the seam along which the whole custody question runs.

### Why it is implicated

AATIP is the only publicly documented instance in which a sitting Senate Majority Leader wrote to a Deputy Secretary of Defense asking for a Restricted SAP with a Bigoted Access List to protect, in his own words, "the methodology used to identify, acquire, study, and engineer the advanced technologies associated with AATIP." Acquire and engineer are custody and reverse-engineering verbs, and Reid put them in a signed letter dated 24 June 2009 to William Lynn III. DIA's own December 2010 info memo from LTG Ronald Burgess to USD(AT&L) confirms the SAP request, confirms DIA refused it in November 2009 for lack of classification grounds, and then confirms Burgess told Reid the work "could be transitioned to another agency or component better suited to oversee the project." That single sentence is the hinge: it is DIA, in writing, describing the handoff that becomes KONA BLUE eighteen months later. Everything the Pentagon has said since 2017 about AATIP has been an argument about the name rather than a denial of the paper trail, and the paper trail is signed by Reid, Lynn, Burgess and Maples.

### The longer account

The origin is documented to the appropriation. LTG Ronald L. Burgess Jr., Director of DIA, wrote to the Under Secretary of Defense for Acquisition, Technology and Logistics in an info memo dated 3 December 2010 (U-10-2552/CE) that "The Advanced Aerospace Threat Identification Program was first sponsored in the Fiscal Year (FY) 2008 Defense Supplemental Appropriation Act by Senators Reid and Inouye with a $10 million add," for the purpose of investigating "foreign advanced aerospace weapon threats from the present out to the next 40 years" and to "build an infrastructure to house a center of expertise on advanced aerospace technologies." The FY2010 Defense Appropriations Act added $12 million more. DIA issued an RFP naming twelve technical areas: lift, propulsion, control, armament, signatures reduction, materials, configuration, power generation, temporal translation, human effects, human interface, and technology integration. The contract went to BAASS, which Burgess notes was "the only bid." A separate DHS memo dated 28 November 2011 puts the two-year total at $22M, the deliverables at 38 research papers, and describes "an active, searchable database (now with greater than 100,000+ entries)."

The procurement history in that DHS memo adds names the DoD record does not volunteer. In May 2008 Senators Reid, Inouye and Stevens invited a DIA program manager (name redacted in the release) to meet with them about a research program involving 'exotic sciences.' In July 2008 LTG Michael Maples, then Director of DIA, was briefed by Reid directly. The redacted program manager is widely identified in secondary literature as James Lacatski; the released document does not name him.

The Reid letter of 24 June 2009 to Deputy Secretary William Lynn III is the load-bearing artifact. Reid asks for a Restricted SAP with a Bigoted Access List for specific portions of AATIP, and enumerates what needs protecting. First, "The methodology used to identify, acquire, study, and engineer the advanced technologies associated with AATIP." Second, allocation of personnel, support and oversight, because "Protection of industry partnerships and participation is critical. Public awareness of an industry's AATIP affiliation may discourage that industry's further participation with the U.S. Government in this program." Third, "Application and engineering", where Reid writes that "The nuanced manner in which some of these technologies will be collected, engineered and applied by the U.S. may require senior level government approval" and that access to "the 'big picture' or overall intent of the program" should be restricted to a strict Bigoted List. Attachment 1 to the letter designates SAP Category "Intelligence, DoD Acquisition," program length FY09-FY13, and lists a preliminary bigoted list beginning with Lynn, Reid, Inouye, and Robert T. Herbert of the U.S. Senate; the contractor list begins with BAASS.

DIA said no. Burgess writes that in November 2009, at the request of the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence, DIA reviewed the program and "determined that, based on classification levels of current and projected deliverables, insufficient grounds existed to classify the program, invoke an ACCM, or establish a restricted SAP." He and Lynn then met Reid in November 2009, "determined the reports were of limited value to DIA," and Burgess suggested transition to another agency. That is the documented push that lands at DHS as KONA BLUE in 2011.

The second life of the name begins in 2017. On 16 December 2017 the New York Times identified AATIP and Luis Elizondo, who had resigned from OUSD(I) effective 4 October 2017. From 2019 onward Pentagon spokeswoman Susan Gough has said Elizondo "had no assigned responsibilities for AATIP." In August 2024, after a three-year FOIA fight, The Black Vault obtained a 2017 draft memorandum authored by Elizondo and intended for issuance by Deputy Secretary Patrick Shanahan. The memo describes AATIP's strategic focus as "beyond next generation technologies in the areas of lift, propulsion, cloaking, and human effects," states that "DoD continues to consider AATIP a priority," and directs that "all DoD elements are hereby required to provide a courtesy copy of any data and information obtained from the field that related to unexplained or unknown aerial systems to the Director of Defense Intelligence for Technical Collection and Special Programs", the position then held by Neill Tipton. DoD had told The Black Vault in December 2021 that no such records existed.

Who matters by name: Reid, Inouye, Stevens; Robert T. Herbert (Reid staff); Lynn; Maples; Burgess; Lacatski (per secondary sourcing); Robert Bigelow and BAASS; Hal Puthoff and Eric Davis as DIRD authors; Elizondo; Tipton; Shanahan; Gough. The record shows an earmarked, contractor-executed technology-exploitation effort whose sponsor asked in writing for engineering-grade compartmentation and was refused. The record does not show DIA ever taking custody of physical material under this contract, and does not resolve whether the OUSD(I) 'AATIP' Elizondo describes was the same activity or a successor riding the same nickname.

### What it has denied

Pentagon spokesperson Susan Gough, in comment to The Black Vault in August 2024: "Luis Elizondo had no assigned responsibilities for AATIP. As noted in the Historical Record Report Volume 1, available on www.aaro.mil, AATIP was never an official, recognized DoD program. The draft, unsigned 'memo for the record' that you reference was crafted by Mr. Elizondo and appears to have been unsolicited." On the FOIA contradiction Gough said only: "As indicated in the response letter to you dated December 14, 2021, regarding 21-F-0964, the initial FOIA search did not yield responsive records." The denial is narrow, attacks the person and the program label, and never addresses the Reid, Lynn or Burgess documents.

Roles the board assigns it: research, funding, reverse-engineering, concealment, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- The DHS memo redacts the name of the DIA program manager who met Reid, Inouye and Stevens in May 2008. Who is it on the unredacted original, and does DIA hold the meeting record?
- Enclosure 1 to the 28 November 2011 O'Toole memo is an 'AATI Program History, dated 23 Nov 2011.' That document has not surfaced. Where is it?
- Reid's letter says government and military personnel 'already involved with this program' should be assigned to support it in a Restricted SAP capacity, with names at Attachment 1 items 5 through 11 redacted. Who are they?
- If DIA refused a restricted SAP in November 2009 for want of classified deliverables, what changed by May 2011 such that DHS S&T was 'informed that there is a body of previous work held by other entities that requires a SAP level classification to access it'? Which entities?
- Was the OUSD(I) activity Elizondo describes a distinct effort from the DIA contract, a residual coordination function, or a renaming? No released document settles this.
- The 38th DIRD was withheld from the FOIA release. What is it?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 20 of 1066, score 108). 16 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 2 official denials recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D3.

### Its strongest connections

- Advanced Aerospace Threat Identification Program (AATIP / AAITP) comes in from **Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program (AAWSAP)** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: AAWSAP is the official name; AATIP/AAITP is Reid's name for it. Elizondo's account is that AATIP grew out of AAWSAP; AARO's 2024 historical report treats 2009 as the standup year for both. The Department has treated them as one contract and two labels, alternately. AARO's report treats 2009 as the standup year for AAWSAP and AATIP and rejects the reverse-engineering account. It is itself a DOPSR-reviewed document, DOPSR-2024-0263. ## KONA BLUE

KONA BLUE is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2011 to 2012; axes C3 A3 D3; 13 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Proposed DHS special access program, circa 2011.

### What it is

KONA BLUE was an unacknowledged, waived Prospective Special Access Program established inside the Department of Homeland Security Science and Technology Directorate by Under Secretary Dr. Tara O'Toole on 11 July 2011, and terminated by Deputy Secretary Jane Holl Lute effective 10 February 2012. Its stated purpose was "to investigate, identify and potentially exploit sensitive advanced aerospace-related technology and to prevent disclosure of scientific, technical and engineering data vital to the US." It was the direct successor proposal to the DIA AAWSAP/AATIP contract and was to be funded by a congressional add steered by Senators Reid and Lieberman.

### Why it is implicated

KONA BLUE is the best-documented attempt in the modern record to build a compartmented U.S. government home for exploitation of materials of claimed non-human origin, and the government declassified the paperwork itself in 2023-2024. The program's own approval memo states DHS proposed a SAP because "we have been informed that there is a body of previous work held by other entities that requires a SAP level classification to access it." That sentence is an admission, in a signed DHS action memo, that someone told the Under Secretary for Science and Technology that SAP-protected prior work existed and was held elsewhere. AARO's cover narrative asserts that interviewees identified KONA BLUE as "a Department of Homeland Security (DHS) sensitive compartment established to protect the retrieval and exploitation of 'non-human biologics'" and then argues the program never got materials because it was never approved. Both can be true and the interesting question survives: who were the "other entities," and what was the "body of previous work"? The termination reason of record is not that the claims were false. Deputy Secretary Lute killed it over adequacy of threat/vulnerability justification and insufficiency of budget and personnel documentation.

### The longer account

The declassified package released jointly by DoD and DHS is unusually complete: an establishment memo, a decision briefing, SAPOC minutes, an action memo, a termination memo, a memorandum for the record, and, folded into the same release, the underlying Reid letter and DIA info memo that explain where the program came from.

Establishment: on 11 July 2011 the Director of DHS S&T's Special Projects Office asked Dr. Tara O'Toole, Under Secretary for Science and Technology and an Original Classification Authority, to approve creation of KONA BLUE as a Prospective Special Access Program under DHS Instruction 140-04-001. The stated purpose was "to investigate, identify and analyze sensitive advanced materials and technologies and to assess potential emerging and/or disruptive scientific and technical data necessary in support of national security." O'Toole approved. Classification was TOP SECRET//KONA BLUE//NOFORN, HANDLE VIA SPECIAL ACCESS CHANNELS ONLY, classified under E.O. reason 1.4(e), with a declassification date of 25 April 2036. The DHS SAPCO decision briefing lists the program manager as Joel Wall, S&T Special Programs Division.

Scope: the proposal document describes four research centers, Data Collection and Analysis, Advanced Technology, Consciousness, and Technology Application/Integration, investigating "emerging and/or disruptive advanced materials and controls, alternative energy/power source/generation opportunities, temporal translation; human effects and interface, signature reduction and weaponry, as well as technology integration." That is the AAWSAP twelve-area list with a Consciousness Center bolted on. The Department's interest area is stated as "the scientific and technical investigation, threat analysis, and assessment of materials and data of unique origin, engineering, and purpose." Materials of unique origin. Written by DHS.

Money: the planned research objectives slide gives FY12 $12M, FY13 $25M, FY14 $35-50M, FY15-FY18 TBD. The SAPCO briefing gives "Expected amount for funding expected to be 12 to 15m" and flags a pending issue: a "Classified Line Item in CSO budget" and that the Under Secretary for Management "has concurred in the need to establish a Business/Administrative SAP to handle primarily 'unacknowledged' monies (e.g., KONA BLUE)." A dedicated financial vehicle for unacknowledged money was being stood up to receive it.

The politics are in the SAPOC minutes of 21 November 2011, chaired by Deputy Secretary Jane Holl Lute, with Caryn Wagner (U/S Intelligence and Analysis), Rafael Borras (U/S Management), Ivan Fong (General Counsel), Gregory Marshall (Chief Security Officer), Steven J. Cover (Chief SAPCO), security officers Michael Zawasky and Allen Olsen, and O'Toole. The minutes record that "The Deputy Secretary opened the meeting with an explanation of what caused this meeting to occur with some sense of urgency which was the impending transfer of funds to DHS from Senators Reid and Leiberman to conduct the KONA BLUE program." O'Toole "stated that the Senators were prepared to provide $10M for the execution of this program in the FY12 budget." She was asked why the FBI was not approached and answered that the FBI "had no organic capability to do research & development in the scientific arena." Wagner raised whether S&T even had collection authority, and whether KONA BLUE "is actually an intelligence program which DIA no longer had the authority to conduct per Executive Order 12333." Lute closed by tasking O'Toole and Cover with a 5-10 page paper documenting "how we got to where we are now with the KONA BLUE program, it's provenance (history) while at DIA, and exactly what science would be researched," adding that "S2 is still not certain of what the 'It' is in the program or otherwise what exactly we are researching."

The November action memo from O'Toole records that in October 2011 she met with Senator Lieberman, Chairman of Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs, and Senate Majority Leader Reid, to discuss program funding. It also states the operative custody claim: "A SAP is proposed as we have been informed that there is a body of previous work held by other entities that requires a SAP level classification to access it."

Death: a progress review was held 8 December 2011 in Lute's office with Fong, Wagner, Cover and Associate General Counsel Kronisch. O'Toole was invited but could not attend. Lute directed immediate termination. The termination memo is dated 12 December 2011 and Lute's signature block shows Disapprove dated 2-10-12. DHS's Acting Deputy Secretary Kristie Canegallo confirmed in a 5 February 2024 letter to Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen Hicks that the PSAP was "terminated on February 10, 2012." A memorandum for the record from Cover dated 5 June 2013 states that no security classification guide, SOP, facility accreditation or AIS plan was ever issued, that all briefed personnel would be administratively debriefed, and that "No other documentation or paperwork would be required."

AARO's framing, published in 2024, is that KONA BLUE "never received any materials or funding" and that "no data or material of any kind was ever transferred to or collected by DHS under the auspices of KONA BLUE." On the narrow question of DHS custody, the paperwork supports that. On the broader question the AARO narrative raises and does not answer: DHS believed material would be delivered, and DHS wrote that a body of prior work was held by other entities under SAP protection. AARO does not identify those entities.

### What it has denied

AARO's position, published as the cover narrative to the 2024 release, is that KONA BLUE 'was a Prospective Special Access Program (PSAP) that had been proposed to DHS leadership but was never approved or formally established,' that it 'never received any materials or funding,' and that 'there is no information beyond the proposal presentation marked with the KONA BLUE name.' The DHS paperwork it accompanies contradicts the 'never established' framing on its face: O'Toole did establish it as a PSAP on 11 July 2011 and Lute had to formally terminate it. AARO's denial is scoped to SAP approval and to DHS custody, not to the existence of the material claims that motivated the proposal.

Roles the board assigns it: reverse-engineering, custody, concealment, funding, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Which 'other entities' held the SAP-level body of previous work referenced in O'Toole's 28 November 2011 memo? The released record never names them.
- The 5-10 page provenance paper Lute tasked O'Toole and Cover to produce has not been released. Was it written?
- The redacted General Counsel remarks in the SAPOC minutes on the rationale for SAP status are the only substantive redaction in the minutes. What did Fong say?
- Was the $10M FY12 congressional add ever appropriated, and if so where did it go after termination on 10 February 2012?
- Who were the individuals administratively debriefed on 5 June 2013, seventeen months after termination, and why the delay?
- AARO says there is 'no information beyond the proposal presentation marked with the KONA BLUE name.' Enclosures 1 through 4 to the O'Toole action memo are referenced but not all released. Where are they?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 10 of 1066, score 122). 16 connections, 9 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 2 official denials recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D3.

## The Wilson-Davis Notes (16 October 2002)

The Wilson-Davis Notes (16 October 2002) is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2002; axes C3 A3 D3; 8 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Fifteen handwritten pages by Eric W. Davis of a conversation with VADM Thomas R. Wilson (ret.).

### What it is

A 15-page document consisting of meeting notes attributed to Dr. Eric W. Davis, headed "Eric Davis Meeting with Adm. Wilson" and dated 10/16/02, purporting to record an approximately seventy-minute conversation with recently retired Vice Adm. Thomas R. Wilson - former Deputy Director of the Defense Intelligence Agency and Assistant Joint Chief of Staff J-2, and Director of DIA from 1999 until July 2002. The notes place the conversation in Wilson's parked car behind the EG&G Special Projects building at Grier and Paradise in Las Vegas. The document is said to have surfaced from the estate of Apollo 14 astronaut Edgar Mitchell following his death in February 2016 and circulated online from 2019. Its provenance is contested and Wilson has denied the meeting on the record. Critical handling note: the version in general circulation is an annotated PDF prepared by an anonymous third party, and the marginal identifications of Lockheed Martin as the unnamed contractor and of Noel Longuemare Jr. as the Senior Review Group chairman are the annotator's inferences, not statements attributed to Wilson anywhere in the notes.

### Why it is implicated

If authentic, this is the single most operationally specific description in the open record of how a legacy retrieval and reverse-engineering effort would be insulated from oversight - and it is specific in ways that are checkable. It describes a special projects record group inside the Office of the Under Secretary of Defense for Acquisition and Technology that sat outside the SAP divisions William Perry reorganised in 1994. It names the officials Wilson allegedly worked through: Perry, Under Secretary Paul Kaminski, Brig. Gen. Michael Kostelnik as director of special programs and SAPCO director and executive secretary to SAPOC, Maj. Gen. H. Marshal Ward, Judith Daly at OUSD(A&T), and Jacques Gansler on arrival in December 1997. It describes a contractor-side committee of program director, security director and corporate attorney - calling itself "the watch committee" - administering access criteria that overrode a serving three-star's statutory need-to-know, and a bigot list dated 1990 and updated 1993 covering 400 to 800 workers containing no politicians, no White House names and no congressional staffers. And it describes the enforcement mechanism: the SAPOC Senior Review Group sustaining the contractor and warning Wilson he would lose his promotion and one or two stars. If it is a fabrication, that is equally load-bearing, because the fabricator understood SAP governance vocabulary well enough that the document was later entered into a congressional hearing record.

### The longer account

The document must be handled as three separable things: an artifact with a physical history, a set of assertions about 1997, and a contested attribution. Keeping them separate is what makes it usable.

As an artifact, its most consequential fact is institutional. The Internet Archive copy of the annotated memo cites, as its provenance, the congressional document HHRG-117-IG05-20220517-SD001 posted at congress.gov under the 17 May 2022 hearing of the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence Subcommittee on Counterterrorism, Counterintelligence and Counterproliferation - the first open congressional UAP hearing in over fifty years. Reporting indicates Rep. Mike Gallagher raised the document with the Pentagon witnesses and obtained unanimous consent to enter it into the record. Whatever its authenticity, it is now a document in a congressional hearing file, and its assertions are therefore assertions Congress has formally received and not adjudicated.

As a set of assertions, the notes describe a search rather than a revelation. Wilson, then Deputy Director of DIA and Assistant JCS J-2, is depicted as acting after an April 1997 Pentagon conference-room briefing given by Steven Greer, Cmdr. Willard Miller and Edgar Mitchell, at which Adm. Mike Crawford and Wilson's superior Lt. Gen. Patrick Hughes were present. Wilson and Miller then spoke privately for two hours about, in the memo's phrasing, UFOs, MJ-12, Roswell, crashed UFOs and alien bodies. Wilson is described as already knowing of intelligence on US military and foreign government close encounters. Over roughly forty-five days he made calls, was pointed by Maj. Gen. Ward and by Perry toward the records groups in OUSD(A&T), located what he describes as a special projects record group that did not belong to the usual SAP divisions as organised by Perry in 1994, and read the index abstracts. He is described as being told by Kostelnik that program security costs ran two to three times the program budget and at times six to seven times core budget, that Perry had wanted an investigation of that ratio and was told to drop it, and that Judith Daly explained the discrepancy as a records-keeping artifact of cumulative versus annual reporting.

What follows is the part that is genuinely novel in the UFO literature, because it is not about a craft. It is about jurisdiction. Wilson is described as making three calls to the program manager, one a conference call with a security director and a corporate attorney, and then flying out ten days later to meet the same three in a secure vault. They call themselves "the watch committee" or gatekeepers, and explain that they formed after an earlier audit investigation nearly exposed them, following which a formal agreement was struck with SAPOC establishing special access criteria administered by the contractor committee rather than by the government, such that no USG personnel gain access unless the contractor's criteria are met "irregardless of the tickets and position USG personnel possessed." Wilson's regulatory and statutory authority as Deputy Director DIA is declared not relevant to their program. He is shown a bigot list several pages long, dated 1990 and updated 1993, mostly program employees, civilians, with a handful of recognisable Pentagon names from OUSD(A&T), one from another department, and one at the NSC who was a Pentagon SES employee. No politicians. No White House. No Congress.

Only then does the substance appear, and it is brief: the program manager states they are not a weapons, intelligence, special operations or logistics program; that they are a reverse engineering program; that something was recovered years ago; that they have an intact craft they believe could fly; that it did not come from overseas and could not have; that they did not know where it was from; that it was "technology that was not of this Earth - not made by man - not by human hands"; that progress had been agonisingly slow with painful lack of outside collaboration; and that the bigot-list workforce ran about 400 to 800 people varying with funding. On Roswell, Holloman, MJ-12 and Zamora and Bentwaters, they declined to discuss. Wilson threatened to escalate to SAPOC, did so, and the Senior Review Group sustained the contractor twice, told him to drop it, and - per the memo - the chairman warned he would not see the DIA director promotion, would get early retirement, and would lose one or two stars. In January 1998 Wilson is described as raising his June 1997 meetings with the new Under Secretary Jacques Gansler, who had somehow been briefed, and who said: "UFOs are real, so-called 'alien abductions' not real."

As a contested attribution, the record is unambiguous on one side and silent on the other. Wilson has denied it directly, telling a reporter it is all fiction, that he would not know Eric Davis if he walked in, and that he did not sit in a car with him in Las Vegas for an hour; he says he told both a Senate staffer and AARO that Davis is making it up. Davis has declined to comment publicly on the document since it appeared. Individuals identified in the memo as arranging the meeting are reported to have denied knowing Wilson. And AARO's own Historical Record Report Volume 1, the government's most extensive published treatment of exactly these allegations, does not contain the name Wilson anywhere in its 1,500-plus lines of text - it disposes of the entire category by asserting that claims involving specific people, locations, tests and documents are inaccurate and largely the product of circular reporting, without engaging this document at all. A critical handling note: the widely circulated PDF is an annotated version, and the marginal identification of Lockheed Martin as the unnamed contractor is the anonymous annotator's inference, not anything attributed to Wilson in the notes.

### What it has denied

Wilson denies both the meeting and its substance in unqualified terms and says he has repeated the denial to a Senate staffer and to AARO. The memo itself anticipates exactly this, recording Wilson saying "If you blow my trust I'll deny meeting you, deny everything said, won't meet with any more people (without clearances) to talk about this topic" - which makes the denial non-diagnostic in either direction. Davis has neither authenticated nor repudiated the document publicly. AARO's most detailed published treatment of the reverse-engineering allegations does not mention Wilson or the memo at all, disposing of the category by reference to circular reporting rather than by addressing the artifact.

Roles the board assigns it: reverse-engineering, concealment, oversight, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- The chain of custody from the alleged 2002 note-taking to the Edgar Mitchell estate to the 2019 online release has never been documented
- Whether an unannotated original bearing Davis's handwriting exists and has been examined by anyone independent
- Whether the congressional submission was accompanied by any authentication, and who submitted it to the committee
- Whether Kostelnik, Ward, Daly, Gansler or Kaminski have ever been asked about the memo's assertions on the record or under oath
- Whether the OUSD(A&T) special projects record group the memo describes exists as an archival entity that could be requested by document control number
- Whether AARO interviewed Wilson, what he said, and why the Historical Record Report does not mention the document at all despite it being in a congressional hearing file
- The identity of the audit investigation the 'watch committee' says nearly exposed them, and the terms of the formal agreement with SAPOC that followed

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 65 of 1066, score 72). 10 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 2 official denials recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** AARO's Historical Record Report Volume 1 (8 March 2024) does not contain the name 'Wilson' anywhere in its text and does not address this document by name, while stating that "AARO found no empirical evidence for claims that the USG and private companies have been reverse-engineering extraterrestrial technology" and that the claim is "in large part, the result of circular reporting from a group of individuals." Source: https://media.defense.gov/2024/Mar/08/2003409233/-1/-1/0/DOPSR-2024-0263-AARO-HISTORICAL-RECORD-REPORT-VOLUME-1-2024.PDF
- **Tier 1 documented.** At the 17 May 2022 House Intelligence subcommittee UAP hearing, DoD witnesses were questioned about the Wilson-Davis memo and stated they were unaware of it. Source: House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence subcommittee hearing, 17 May 2022; contemporaneous reporting
- **Tier 1 documented.** Brig. Gen. Steven Lovekin is not in the published notes. All fifteen pages were read image by image: Wilson, Davis, Steven Greer, Will Miller and Edgar Mitchell are in them and Lovekin is not. Source: The Wilson-Davis notes as published, all fifteen pages, https://documents2.theblackvault.com/casefiles/wilsonleak.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Internet Archive copy of the annotated memo identifies its provenance as "Part of the US Congressional Record" at https://www.congress.gov/117/meeting/house/114761/documents/HHRG-117-IG05-20220517-SD001.pdf, the document file for the 17 May 2022 House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence subcommittee UAP hearing. Reporting indicates Rep. Mike Gallagher raised the document at that hearing and obtained unanimous consent to enter it into the record as SD001. Source: https://archive.org/details/annotated_wilson_memo
- **Tier 1 documented.** The document is dated 10/16/02, headed "Eric Davis Meeting with Adm. Wilson," and records the meeting as taking place at the EG&G Special Projects building at Grier and Paradise, with the conversation held in Wilson's car behind the EG&G building until 11:20 A.M. Source: Wilson-Davis memo, 16 October 2002, p.1; full text at https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The memo describes three contractor officials - a program director, a security director identified as NSA-retired and a counterintelligence expert, and a corporate attorney - who "Called themselves 'the watch committee,' or gate keepers," and who told Wilson that access criteria were "to be administered by contractor committee... irregardless of the tickets and position USG personnel possessed," and that "Regulatory and statutory authority as Deputy Director DIA not relevant or pertinent to nature of their program." Source: Wilson-Davis memo, 16 October 2002, pp.10-12; https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The memo names Maj. Gen. H. Marshal Ward and Secretary William Perry as the sources of the suggestion to search records-group files in OUSD(A&T), and identifies Paul Kaminski as Under Secretary of Defense (Acquisition and Technology) and Brig. Gen. Michael Kostelnik as director of special programs in OUSD(A&T), director of the Special Access Programs Coordination Office (SAPCO), executive secretary to the Special Access Programs Oversight Committee (SAPOC), and a member of the Senior Review Group. The memo states that what Wilson found there was "a special projects record group not belonging to usual SAP - a special subset of the unacknowledged/carve-outs/waived programs - not belonging to usual SAP divisions as organized in '94 by Perry himself - set apart from rest but buried/covered by conventional SAPs." Source: Wilson-Davis memo, 16 October 2002, pp.6-7; https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The memo package includes an attached letter from Cmdr. Willard 'Will' Miller dated 25 April 2002, addressed to "Dr. Eric W. Davis, CEO, Warp Drive Metrics, 4849 San Rafael Ave., Las Vegas, NV 89120," offering to supply "A list of civilian government contractors who, by virtue of their past and current highly classified work... most likely have current involvement in and knowledge of USG work in alien-derived technologies, crashes, landing, and associated events" at "$180 per straight-time hour." Source: Miller to Davis, 25 April 2002, reproduced in the Wilson-Davis memo package, pp.3-5; https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The memo records Wilson identifying the holder only as "An aerospace technology contractor - one of the top ones in US," "the best one of them," with intelligence "In their corporate portfolio," and refusing to name it, the program compartment or the code name, each time on the grounds that it was a "core secret." Source: Wilson-Davis memo, 16 October 2002, pp.8-9; https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The memo records Wilson quoting incoming Under Secretary of Defense (Acquisition and Technology) Jacques Gansler, in a January 1998 conversation about the June 1997 meetings, as saying "UFOs are real, so-called 'alien abductions' not real," and then telling Wilson to drop the matter. Source: Wilson-Davis memo, 16 October 2002, p.14; https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The memo records a bigot list "several pages long - dated 1990, updated 1993," comprising mostly civilian program employees, containing no politicians, no White House names, no President, no Congressional people and no Congressional staffers, but including "a handful of names" of Pentagon individuals Wilson recognised - some from OUSD(A&T), one from another department and one at the National Security Council who was a Pentagon SES employee - and a workforce of "about 400-800 (bigot list count) workers varying in number with funding or personnel changes." Source: Wilson-Davis memo, 16 October 2002, pp.11-13; https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The memo records that after Wilson complained to the SAPOC Senior Review Group, its members sustained the contractor's access denial twice, told him "to immediately drop the matter and let it go," and that the Senior Review Group chairman warned that if he did not comply he "would not see Director DIA promotion, get early retirement, lose 1 or 2 stars along the way." Source: Wilson-Davis memo, 16 October 2002, pp.13-14; https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The memo records that program security spending ran "like 2-3 times the program budget, but there were times when it went as high as 6-7 times core budget," that Perry wanted an investigation of the ratio and "was told to drop it," and names Judith Daly, Assistant Deputy Under Secretary of Defense for Advanced Development, OUSD(A&T), as the official who explained the figures as cumulative rather than annual security budget reporting. Source: Wilson-Davis memo, 16 October 2002, pp.7-8; https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The memo records the contractor program manager stating the program was "a reverse engineering program," that "They had a craft - an intact craft they believed could fly," and that "it was technology that was not of this Earth - not made by man - not by human hands." Source: Wilson-Davis memo, 16 October 2002, pp.12-13; https://archive.org/stream/annotated_wilson_memo/annotated_wilson_memo_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The notes give the correspondent no rank, no service and no first name beyond 'Will', and his letter carries no rank block, so the 'Cdr. Willard Miller, USN' form used across the podcast corpus is not supported by the document it is drawn from. In the letter he writes: 'Although I have held a Top Secret (TS) clearance with access to Special Compartmented Information (SCI), and other "special" clearances for "other" programs, I currently do not work in the classified realm, nor do I hold those clearances.' Source: The Wilson-Davis notes as published, pp. 3 to 4, https://documents2.theblackvault.com/casefiles/wilsonleak.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The published document is typed, not handwritten, and two of its fifteen pages are somebody else's letter. The copy in public circulation is fifteen typed pages headed 'EWD NOTES', then '10/16/02', then 'Eric Davis Meeting with Adm. Wilson', numbered 1 to 15 at the foot. Pages 3 and 4 are a two page letter on United States Navy letterhead dated April 25, 2002, from Belleair Bluffs, Florida, to 'Dr. Eric W. Davis, CEO, Warp Drive Metrics', signed 'Will Miller'; the notes flag it at the foot of page 2 as '[paper-clipped letter here]'. Source: The Wilson-Davis notes as published, fifteen pages, read image by image, https://documents2.theblackvault.com/casefiles/wilsonleak.pdf
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Adm. Thomas Wilson has denied the meeting on the record, telling a reporter "It's all fiction" and "I wouldn't know Eric Davis if he walked in right now... I don't remember him, and I definitely did not sit with him in a car for an hour in Las Vegas," and states he told both a Senate staffer and AARO that Davis is making it all up. Eric Davis has declined to comment publicly on the document. Source: https://lifeinjonestown.substack.com/p/reviving-the-w-d-memo
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Eric W. Davis has confirmed to journalists that the handwritten notes are his own. Source: Eric W. Davis, statements to journalists following the 2019 emergence of the notes
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The notes record Wilson describing a 1997 attempt to gain access to a special access programme holding recovered non-human material in contractor custody, and being refused on need-to-know grounds by a programme security officer, a contractor programme manager and a lawyer. Source: The Wilson-Davis notes, 16 Oct 2002, in public circulation since 2019
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Thomas R. Wilson denies that the meeting took place and denies the substance attributed to him. Source: Thomas R. Wilson, statements to journalists, 2019 onward

Sources: American Alchemy - Weinstein/Davis episode, https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xnxasfyHtfo, dated 2026-03-08.
- The Wilson-Davis Notes (16 October 2002) comes in from **Thomas R. Wilson** (person, board tier C), a denial link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: Wilson denies the meeting and the substance attributed to him. Wilson's denial is recorded as a primary artefact. It is irreconcilable with Davis's confirmation of authorship, and no agency has attempted to resolve it. Sources: The Debrief, 'Summer of the Saucers' (notes Wilson-Davis Memo raised at the 17 May 2022 House Intelligence UAP hearing), https://thedebrief.org/summer-of-the-saucers/, dated 2022-08-01.
- The Wilson-Davis Notes (16 October 2002) runs out to **David Charles Grusch** (person, board tier A), a witness-chain link at Tier 3 stated inference. The link reads: The 2002 notes and the 2023 sworn testimony describe the same access-denial mechanism. Both describe an appropriately cleared official being refused a read-on by programme security officers and contractor gatekeepers on need-to-know grounds. Twenty-one years apart, two officials describe the same wall. Sources: The Debrief, 'Summer of the Saucers' (notes Wilson-Davis Memo raised at the 17 May 2022 House Intelligence UAP hearing), https://thedebrief.org/summer-of-the-saucers/, dated 2022-08-01; AARO, 'Report on the Historical Record of U.S. Government Involvement with Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena (UAP), Volume I' (DOPSR-2024-0263), https://media.defense.gov/2024/Mar/08/2003409233/-1/-1/0/DOPSR-2024-0263-AARO-HISTORICAL-RECORD-REPORT-VOLUME-1-2024.PDF, dated 2024-03-08.
- The Wilson-Davis Notes (16 October 2002) runs out to **EG&G (Edgerton, Germeshausen and Grier)** (institution, board tier A), a custody link at Tier 3 stated inference. The link reads: The meeting is reported to have taken place at an EG&G facility in Las Vegas. EG&G held the Groom Lake support and personnel-transport contracts; the reported venue matters because it places the conversation inside the contractor perimeter it describes. Sources: The Debrief, 'Summer of the Saucers' (notes Wilson-Davis Memo raised at the 17 May 2022 House Intelligence UAP hearing), https://thedebrief.org/summer-of-the-saucers/, dated 2022-08-01.

# 2017-2026: Post-disclosure

## To The Stars Academy of Arts & Science Inc. (later To The Stars Inc.)

To The Stars Academy of Arts & Science Inc. (later To The Stars Inc.) is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2017; axes C3 A2 D3; 17 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Delaware public benefit corporation, SEC CIK 1710274.

### What it is

To The Stars Academy of Arts and Science Inc. was a Delaware public benefit corporation incorporated in 2017 and registered with the SEC under Commission File Number 024-10728, CIK 1710274, EIN 82-0601064. Its directors were Tom DeLonge, former CIA Directorate of Operations officer Jim Semivan, and physicist Harold Puthoff; Lockheed Skunk Works veteran Steve Justice and former Pentagon official Luis Elizondo were listed as significant employees. It raised money through a Regulation A offering, hosted the first officially released US military UAP videos, and signed a research agreement with the US Army before winding down its aerospace work and reverting to entertainment as To The Stars Inc.

### Why it is implicated

TTSA matters to this map not as a research institution but as a legal vehicle. For roughly three years it was the corporate person through which men carrying intelligence, special-access and black-aerospace careers could act in public, hold material, sign agreements with the government, and speak to Congress and the press without a government employer's prepublication apparatus sitting on top of every sentence. Its SEC filings are the single richest primary record on this board of who those people were, what clearances and career histories they carried, and what the venture was actually organised to do. In October 2019 it converted that standing into a Cooperative Research and Development Agreement with the US Army Combat Capabilities Development Command covering 'material science, space-time metric engineering, quantum physics, beamed energy propulsion, and active camouflage' - a government laboratory relationship with a private company that held claimed anomalous material. AARO's own history records the same personnel network attempting to reconstitute the AAWSAP effort inside the Department of Homeland Security as the prospective special access programme KONA BLUE.

### The longer account

To The Stars Academy is the most thoroughly documented entity on this board, for a reason that has nothing to do with UAP: it sold securities. A company that offers stock under Regulation A must file with the SEC, and the filings are public, dated, signed and auditable. The FY2017 Form 1-K is therefore the closest thing this subject has to a sworn organisational chart.

What it shows is a very small company with a very unusual staff. Officers and directors were Tom DeLonge as president and interim chief executive; Jim Semivan as vice president of operations and director; and Harold E. Puthoff as vice president of science and technology and director. The filing states that Semivan 'retired from the Central Intelligence Agency's Directorate of Operations after 25 years working as an operations officer both overseas and domestically,' that he 'was a member of the CIA's Senior Intelligence Service,' and that he 'currently holds Top Secret Clearances.' It states that Puthoff is president and chief executive of EarthTech International and director of the Institute for Advanced Studies at Austin. Two significant employees are listed: Steve Justice, Director of Advanced Programs and Technology, part-time at twenty hours a week, whose industry background the filing ties to the Lockheed organisation 'known as the "Skunk Works"'; and Luis Elizondo, Chief of Security and Special Programs, full-time, described as a career intelligence officer. As of 31 December 2017 the company had eleven employees.

The financial picture in the same filing is stark. Revenues for 2017 were $1,376,215 against cost of revenues of $750,932, producing gross profit of $625,283 - essentially a merchandise and music business. The net loss for 2017 was $30,665,436, against $422,670 the prior year, almost entirely non-cash stock compensation. Gravity Holdings LLC, whose sole member is the DeLonge Family Trust, held 60,000,000 Class A shares, 85.7 per cent of the class. This was not a research budget. It was a capital structure wrapped around a founder and a small group of named individuals.

What the company did with that structure is the part that belongs on this map. It became, as the filing puts it, 'the very first company to obtain official U.S. Government footage of unidentified aerial phenomena ("UAP") that had gone through the declassification process and been approved for public release,' publishing the footage through its online Community of Interest. The videos that entered the mainstream through the New York Times in December 2017 - the material now known as FLIR1, Gimbal and GoFast - reached the public through a private securities issuer, not through a Pentagon press release. That routing is itself a finding. A declassification review was completed and the product was handed to a company in which a serving-until-recently Pentagon official was chief of security and special programs.

On 17 October 2019 TTSA announced a Cooperative Research and Development Agreement with the US Army Combat Capabilities Development Command 'to advance TTSA's materiel and technology innovations in order to develop enhanced capabilities for Army ground vehicles.' The announcement described the technology set as leveraging 'developments in material science, space-time metric engineering, quantum physics, beamed energy propulsion, and active camouflage,' and set out the exchange: 'TTSA will share its discoveries with Ground Vehicle System Center (GVSC) and Ground Vehicle Survivability and Protection (GVSP) and the U.S. Army shall provide laboratories, expertise, support, and resources to help characterize the technologies and its applications.' Dr Joseph Cannon of US Army Futures Command called the partnership 'an exciting, non-traditional source for novel materials and transformational technologies.' Steve Justice, by then TTSA's chief operating officer and aerospace division director, said the agreement brought 'additional, critically important expertise that is necessary to advance the state-of-the-art in both our near and long-term technology areas of study.'

Read against the rest of this board, the CRADA is the important document. A private company holding material it described as anomalous obtained access to Army laboratories to characterise it, under an instrument that is not a procurement contract and does not require the government to pay. A CRADA moves capability and data across the government-private boundary in both directions with far less contractual and oversight furniture than a prime contract, and it does so without a competitive solicitation. Whether or not anything anomalous was ever in those laboratories, the mechanism is precisely the kind of low-visibility government-private research channel this board is built to map.

The network did not begin with TTSA and did not end with it. AARO's Historical Record Report records that after AAWSAP was terminated, 'its supporters unsuccessfully attempted to convince DHS to support a new version of this effort dubbed KONA BLUE,' which AARO characterises as 'a UAP-related Prospective Special Access Program (PSAP) called KONA BLUE that was proposed to the Department of Homeland Security (DHS) and supported by individuals who believed the USG was hiding off-world technology,' and which 'was never approved by DHS.' The same people, in sequence: a DIA contract, a proposed DHS special access programme, a Delaware public benefit corporation, a congressional hearing.

Elizondo, Christopher Mellon and Justice departed in December 2020, the aerospace and materials work wound down, and the entity renamed itself To The Stars Inc. and returned to entertainment. What it leaves behind is a documentary trail that is unusually hard to argue with, because it was filed under penalty of securities law: names, ages, clearances, prior employers, share counts, losses, and an executed agreement with a US Army laboratory command.

### What it has denied

TTSA has not been the subject of an official denial addressed to it by name. The nearest thing in the record is AARO's 2024 finding that a sample it acquired 'from a private UAP investigating organization and the U.S. Army' is a manufactured terrestrial alloy of magnesium, zinc and bismuth with no exceptional qualities, and its characterisation of KONA BLUE - proposed by the same personnel network - as a prospective special access programme that DHS never approved and whose supporters 'never provided empirical evidence to support their claims.'

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, custody, reverse-engineering.

### Open questions on this node

- The full executed CRADA between TTSA and the Army Combat Capabilities Development Command has not been released; its statement of work, data rights, term and any material transfer provisions are unknown.
- Whether the sample AARO says it obtained 'from a private UAP investigating organization and the U.S. Army' is the same material that moved under the TTSA CRADA is not established by any document reviewed here.
- The route by which the FLIR1, Gimbal and GoFast videos passed prepublication review and reached a private securities issuer rather than a government release channel is not documented in the public record.
- What happened to TTSA's materials holdings when the aerospace and science divisions wound down in 2020-2022, and who holds them now.
- Whether any TTSA principal was under a live nondisclosure or special-access indoctrination instrument at the time of the company's public statements, and whether DoD SAPCO approval under DoDD 5205.07 Section 3.1.g was sought or given for any briefing to Congress.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 51 of 1066, score 78). 18 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A2/D3.

## CRADA between U.S. Army Ground Vehicle Systems Center and To The Stars Academy

CRADA between U.S. Army Ground Vehicle Systems Center and To The Stars Academy is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2019 to 2023; axes C3 A1 D3; 10 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Cooperative Research and Development Agreement, signed 10 October 2019.

### What it is

Cooperative Research and Development Agreement No. 19-15, titled 'Novel & Emerging Technology Exploitation (NETE),' between To The Stars Academy of Arts and Science, Inc. and the U.S. Army Combat Capabilities Development Command Ground Vehicle Systems Center at the Detroit Arsenal, Warren, Michigan. Executed under 15 U.S.C. section 3710a with a term of sixty months from the effective date, it committed TTSA to provide the Army with samples of metamaterial 'collected, obtained, or developed as part of its Field Operations' and the Army to provide laboratories, expertise and analysis, with no exchange of funds in either direction. TTSA's principal investigator of record was Luis Elizondo.

### Why it is implicated

This is the only publicly released, signed U.S. Government instrument in which a federal laboratory agrees to characterize material that the counterparty represented, in filings to the Securities and Exchange Commission, as coming from unidentified aerial phenomena. That makes it the board's hardest documentary bridge between the alleged recovery-and-exploitation enterprise and the ordinary federal technology-transfer system. The Joint Work Plan names the Government's actual interests, and the list is not generic materials science: 'inertial mass reduction, mechanical/structural metamaterials, electromagnetic metamaterial wave guides, quantum physics, quantum communications, and beamed energy propulsion.' Inertial mass reduction is not a ground-vehicle survivability requirement. The Collaborator principal investigator is Luis Elizondo, the former Office of the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence official associated with AATIP who left DoD in 2017 alleging excessive secrecy, meaning the same individual who publicly accused the Pentagon of concealment is, two years later, the named point of contact delivering anomalous material back into an Army laboratory under an agreement whose supporting records the Department of Defense then withheld under national-defense FOIA exemptions. Finally, the CRADA form itself is the point: it moves technology and creates invention rights without appropriated funds, without a contract, without competition and without a reportable obligation, and it carries a statutory information shield. It is a legal channel for moving exploitable material into and out of the Government that leaves almost no financial footprint.

### The longer account

On 17 October 2019 To The Stars Academy of Arts & Science issued a press release announcing a Cooperative Research and Development Agreement with the U.S. Army Combat Capabilities Development Command to develop enhanced capabilities for Army ground vehicles. The release was thin. The Army was not. Doug Halleaux, public affairs officer for CCDC's Ground Vehicle Systems Center, gave John Greenewald of The Black Vault the executed agreement, twenty-six pages, redacted only for phone numbers.

The document is CRADA No. 19-15, 'Novel & Emerging Technology Exploitation (NETE),' between TTSA and CCDC Ground Vehicle Systems Center, 6501 E. 11 Mile Road, Warren, Michigan. The named administrators matter. On the Government side the principal investigators are Ben Soave and Dr. Joseph Cannon, both of Ground Vehicle Survivability and Protection, mail stop 263b. The ORTA representative is Andrea Simon; Government legal counsel is Christina Dellmore, Chief of the Intellectual Property Law Division at US Army TACOM LCMC. On the Collaborator side, the principal investigator and preferred contact is Luis Elizondo, 'Director of Global Security and Special Programs,' Encinitas, California. Collaborator legal counsel is Heidy Vaquerano, Esq., of Wolf, Rifkin, Shapiro, Schulman & Rabkin, LLP, Los Angeles.

The Joint Work Plan is where the agreement stops sounding like armor research. The Government's stated interests are 'a variety of the Collaborator's technologies, such as, but not limited to inertial mass reduction, mechanical/structural metamaterials, electromagnetic metamaterial wave guides, quantum physics, quantum communications, and beamed energy propulsion.' The Collaborator's deliverables include, verbatim, 'Metamaterial: Samples of mechanical and EM sensitive metamaterial collected, obtained, or developed as part of its Field Operations,' plus written material analysis and quantum-communication research. 'Field Operations' is the operative euphemism; the CRADA does not define provenance, and the Army made a point of saying provenance was irrelevant to it.

The legal architecture is standard Federal Technology Transfer Act plumbing put to non-standard use. Information exchanged is protected under 15 U.S.C. section 3710a(c)(7), and every page carries a marking legend invoking it. Licenses to CRADA inventions run under 15 U.S.C. section 3710a(b)(2), with the Collaborator holding an option for an exclusive license that must be requested within six months of notification of an invention. The agreement automatically expires sixty months from the effective date under Article 4.2, and enters force on the date signed by the last authorized representative under Article 4.3.

TTSA's own SEC filings supply the numbers the CRADA does not. In its Regulation A offering circular the company states that on 10 October 2019 it entered into the CRADA with CCDC, that 'the purpose of the CRADA is to advance developments in material science (including testing of the metamaterials), space-time metric engineering, quantum physics, beamed energy propulsion and active camouflage,' that the parties bear their own costs, that the total value of anticipated U.S. Government resources including personnel, services, intellectual property, facilities, equipment and reporting is 'estimated at $750,000,' that the total value of the company's participation is 'estimated at $1,000,000,' and that the agreement is terminable by mutual consent or unilaterally on thirty days' notice.

The same filing describes where the material came from. The ADAM Research Project, Acquisition and Data Analysis of Materials, launched in July 2018, and 'within one year this division acquired a collection of material samples reported to come from advanced vehicles of unknown origin from all over the world.' In July 2019 the company 'purchased multiple pieces of metamaterials and an archive of initial analysis and research.' Elsewhere TTSA states flatly to the SEC: 'The exotic materials that were purchased come from unidentified aerial phenomenon and are anomalous in that they have a structure and composition that we believe are unknown to any existing commercial or military application.' By November 2019, per the filing, ETI had performed analysis pursuant to the SOW-MSSA showing 'a unique physical structure unknown to any past or current commercial or military application.' The filing also records that planned ADAM laboratory testing 'was paused.'

The Army's public framing was deliberately deflationary. Halleaux told The Black Vault that 'the Army is providing the expertise and facilities to analyze the materials, and TTSA is providing the materials themselves; both parties receive the results of the analysis,' and that 'just as there is no financial compensation to TTSA for the use of the materials, there is no financial compensation to the Army for the use of the facilities.' On provenance he was explicit: 'the Army's interest is in the potential for novel materials and exploring the edges of materials science, any speculation as to their origin is (pardon the pun) immaterial.' He also stressed that a CRADA is not an endorsement, offering the Army's fuel-cell CRADA with General Motors as the comparison.

Then the record closed. Individuals who had custody of pieces of the metamaterial before TTSA acquired them were placed under non-disclosure agreements; The Black Vault reached one such person, who declined comment citing an NDA. Some of the material was described in SEC filings as being 'on loan.' The Department of Defense invoked FOIA exemptions covering national defense and foreign policy to withhold CRADA-related documents. It took until October 2024, five years after execution and at the natural end of the sixty-month term, before the Army agreed to a rolling release under FOIA case FA-24-0006, with the first package released on 23 October 2024.

The analytic point for the board is the instrument, not the rock. A CRADA requires no appropriation, no competition, no contract file and no reportable obligation, and carries a statutory information shield. It creates a lawful, low-visibility conduit for moving materials of undeclared origin into a federal laboratory and moving analysis and invention rights back out, with the collaborator holding a first option on exclusive rights to anything invented. Whatever the metamaterial turns out to be, CRADA 19-15 is the working proof that the channel exists, is routine, and is FOIA-resistant by design.

### What it has denied

The Army's posture is that CRADA 19-15 is unremarkable technology transfer, that a CRADA is not an endorsement mechanism, that the arrangement is directly comparable to the Army's fuel-cell CRADA with General Motors, and that the origin of the materials is 'immaterial' to the Army's interest. That posture sits badly against the Joint Work Plan's explicit interest in inertial mass reduction and beamed energy propulsion, and against DoD's later invocation of national-defense and foreign-policy FOIA exemptions to withhold records about an agreement it publicly characterized as routine and unclassified.

Roles the board assigns it: reverse-engineering, custody, research, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- What is the chain of custody for the metamaterial before TTSA's July 2019 purchase, who sold it, and what is in the 'archive of initial analysis and research' acquired with it?
- Who signed CRADA 19-15 for each party, and on what date did the last authorized representative sign, fixing the Effective Date?
- What tests did the Army actually perform, what did they find, and what does the close-out report say?
- Who or what is 'ETI,' the entity that performed the November 2019 analysis pursuant to the SOW-MSSA referenced in TTSA's SEC filing?
- Did any CRADA Invention arise, and did TTSA exercise its six-month exclusive-license option under 15 U.S.C. section 3710a(b)(2)?
- Why did DoD apply national-defense and foreign-policy FOIA exemptions to records of an agreement the Army publicly described as unclassified routine materials research?
- Are there other CRADAs between federal laboratories and private parties covering material of undeclared or anomalous provenance, and can they be enumerated through ORTA records?
- What happened to the physical samples at expiration of the sixty-month term: returned, retained, consumed in testing, or transferred?
- Who are the individuals under NDA to TTSA regarding provenance, and what does the NDA prohibit?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 92 of 1066, score 64). 11 connections, 5 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C3/A1/D3.
