## FY2022 NDAA Sec. 1683 (P.L. 117-81; 50 U.S.C. 3373)

FY2022 NDAA Sec. 1683 (P.L. 117-81; 50 U.S.C. 3373) is a legislation on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2021; axes C1 A3 D2; 8 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: Gillibrand's founding authority for AARO.

### What it is

Section 1683 of the National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2022, Public Law 117-81, enacted 27 December 2021 at 135 Stat. 2118, is the statute that created the office lineage running from the Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force through the Airborne Object Identification and Management Synchronization Group to the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office. It is codified at 50 U.S.C. 3373 and was rewritten wholesale a year later by section 6802(a) of the Intelligence Authorization Act for FY2023 (Public Law 117-263), and amended again in 2023 and 2025.

### Why it is implicated

This is the only place in the United States Code where Congress wrote down, in operative statutory language, that programs concerning unidentified anomalous phenomena may have been 'protected by restricted access' and 'not been explicitly and clearly reported to Congress,' and demanded an accounting of them going back to 1 January 1945. It is also the only statute that directs the Secretary of Defense to designate line organizations for 'the testing of materials, medical studies, and development of theoretical models' relating to UAP, and to run a science plan whose stated purpose includes providing 'the foundation for potential future investments to replicate' anomalous performance characteristics. Congress did not merely authorize a reporting office; it authorized materials testing, medical study of exposed personnel, and replication research, the exact functional description of a reverse-engineering enterprise, and then required that any prior version of the same enterprise be disclosed. The statute's subsequent amendment history is a record of the Executive branch failing to comply.

### The longer account

Section 1683 began as a modest reorganization and became, through two rewrites, the sharpest disclosure instrument in federal law. Read it as a chronology of what Congress learned.

The original enactment on 27 December 2021 stood up an office to absorb the Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force, in the wake of the June 2021 ODNI preliminary assessment. A year later, section 6802(a) of the Intelligence Authorization Act for FY2023 amended the section generally, replacing it in full, and section 1673(c)(1) of the same law substituted 'anomalous' for 'aerial' wherever the word appeared. That single-word substitution is not cosmetic. It extended the statute's reach from aircraft to transmedium objects and submerged objects, a definitional move that matters if what is at issue includes things recovered from water.

The rewritten statute names the office the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office and hardwires a dual chain of command. The Director is appointed by the Secretary of Defense in consultation with the DNI; the Deputy Director is appointed by the DNI in coordination with the Secretary. The Director reports directly to the Deputy Secretary of Defense and the Principal Deputy Director of National Intelligence, and, separately, 'to the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security on all administrative matters.' That administrative reporting line runs to the same office that is a SAP Oversight Authority under DoDI 5205.11 and the DoD interface to the DNI's Controlled Access Program Central Office. The office chartered to find concealed compartments is administratively subordinate, in part, to the office that manages compartments.

Subsection (j) is the load-bearing clause. Within 540 days of 23 December 2022, the Director was to deliver to the defense committees, the intelligence committees and congressional leadership a written report on 'the historical record of the United States Government relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena,' drawn from intelligence community records, oral history interviews, open source analysis, interviews of current and former officials, and classified and unclassified national archives. It must focus on the period beginning 1 January 1945. And it must include 'a compilation and itemization of the key historical record of the involvement of the intelligence community with unidentified anomalous phenomena, including, (I) any program or activity that was protected by restricted access that has not been explicitly and clearly reported to Congress; (II) successful or unsuccessful efforts to identify and track unidentified anomalous phenomena; and (III) any efforts to obfuscate, manipulate public opinion, hide, or otherwise provide incorrect unclassified or classified information about unidentified anomalous phenomena or related activities.' Subsection (j)(2) obliges the Archivist of the United States to make classified NARA holdings available to the office for that purpose.

Subsections (e) and (g) are where the statute stops sounding like transparency legislation and starts sounding like a program charter. Under (e)(1), the Secretary of Defense in coordination with the DNI 'shall designate one or more line organizations that will be primarily responsible for scientific, technical, and operational analysis of data gathered by field investigations... including with respect to the testing of materials, medical studies, and development of theoretical models.' Under (e)(2), both officials must issue directives ensuring those line organizations 'has authority to draw on the special expertise of persons outside the Federal Government with appropriate security clearances.' Under (g), the science plan must account for performance 'that exceed the known state of the art in science or technology, including in the areas of propulsion, aerodynamic control, signatures, structures, materials, sensors, countermeasures, weapons, electronics, and power generation,' and 'provide the foundation for potential future investments to replicate or otherwise better understand any such advanced characteristics and performance.' Congress wrote replication into the law. Subsection (k)(1)(B)(xi) then requires each annual report to include 'An update on any efforts underway on the ability to capture or exploit discovered unidentified anomalous phenomena.'

Definitions matter here too. Subsection (n)(6) defines 'line organization' to exclude any component of the Office of the Secretary of Defense, pushing materials testing and medical studies out to service laboratories, agencies and national laboratories, and therefore out to contractor-operated facilities. Subsection (n)(7) defines transmedium objects as those observed transitioning between space and atmosphere, or atmosphere and water.

The amendment history is the compliance record. In December 2023, P.L. 118-31 §7341 moved the annual report obligation from the DNI and SecDef jointly onto the AARO Director alone. In December 2025, P.L. 119-60 §1672(b) rewrote subsection (f)(1)(A) so the data-availability mandate binds 'each element of the intelligence community and component of the Department of Defense', the prior text covered only IC elements. Congress had to amend the statute to reach the Department of Defense's own components. Section 1671 added the NORAD/NORTHCOM intercept requirement with a 2004 lookback, and section 1673(a) demanded an accounting of the security classification guides themselves. Each amendment closes a door someone had walked through.

### What it has denied

The statute contains no denial; the office it created does. AARO's Volume I historical record report (8 March 2024) states it found no verifiable evidence that the U.S. government or private industry has ever had access to extraterrestrial technology, and attributes whistleblower accounts to misidentification of authentic classified programs. That formulation concedes the existence of authentic classified programs proximate to the allegations while denying their nature, and it is the denial Chairwoman Luna attacked on the record on 9 September 2025.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, research, reverse-engineering, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Which line organizations were designated under subsections (d) and (e), and for which functions? The statute requires them to be named in annual reports; no unclassified naming has been published.
- Was the science plan required by subsection (g) ever produced, and does it contain a replication program element as the statute contemplates?
- Has any annual report actually addressed subsection (k)(1)(B)(xi), efforts 'to capture or exploit' discovered UAP, and what was said?
- Did the Archivist open classified NARA holdings to AARO under subsection (j)(2), and what accession records document that transfer?
- Why did Congress need to amend subsection (f)(1)(A) in December 2025 to reach DoD components, which components had withheld data under the prior text?
- The original 27 December 2021 text of section 1683 differs materially from the 2022 rewrite; what provisions in the original were dropped, and at whose request? The Code preserves only a summary amendment note.
- Has the AARO Director delivered the accounting of security classification guides required within 180 days of 18 December 2025, and does it list any guide covering recovered materials?

## ADAM Research Project (Acquisition and Data Analysis of Materials)

ADAM Research Project (Acquisition and Data Analysis of Materials) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2018 to 2021; axes C3 A1 D2; 7 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: TTSA materials program run with EarthTech International.

### What it is

The ADAM Research Project, Acquisition and Data Analysis of Materials, is a private materials-analysis program launched in July 2018 by To The Stars Academy of Arts & Science, a Delaware public benefit corporation founded by Tom DeLonge. It acquires physical samples reportedly recovered from advanced aerospace vehicles of unknown origin and contracts laboratory analysis, principally through Hal Puthoff's EarthTech International in Austin, Texas. Its best-known holding is the magnesium-zinc-bismuth 'Art's Parts' sample purchased with an archive from journalist Linda Moulton Howe in July 2019.

### Why it is implicated

ADAM is the only publicly documented instance of claimed recovered material moving from private custody into a federal laboratory pipeline with a signed government agreement attached. In October 2019 TTSA announced a Cooperative Research and Development Agreement with the U.S. Army Combat Capabilities Development Command covering material science, space-time metric engineering, beamed energy propulsion and active camouflage. By 2024 the same magnesium-zinc-bismuth sample had been analyzed at Oak Ridge National Laboratory with AARO in the room, and Puthoff and Eric Davis, both of whom authored deliverables under the DIA AAWSAP contract a decade earlier, wrote the private counter-analysis. That is a closed loop: the same physicists who wrote the government's exotic-propulsion reference documents in 2009-2010 are the ones adjudicating a national laboratory's findings on a claimed recovered artifact in 2024. Whether or not the sample is exotic, the custody chain and the recurrence of the same named individuals across government and private roles is the finding.

### The longer account

TTSA announced ADAM in July 2018 as an academic research program focused on the collection and scientific evaluation of material samples obtained through reported encounters with advanced aerospace vehicles of unknown origin. Analysis was contracted to EarthTech International, the Austin think tank run by Dr. Harold E. Puthoff, who was simultaneously TTSA's Vice President of Science and Technology and had been a senior adviser and subcontractor on the DIA-funded AAWSAP/AATIP work.

The acquisition that gave ADAM an object rather than a concept came on 25 July 2019. TTSA's press release states that it 'has acquired multiple pieces of metamaterials and an archive of initial analysis and research for their controversial ADAM Research Project,' materials 'previously retained and studied by investigative journalist Linda Moulton Howe and are reported to have come from an advanced aerospace vehicle of unknown origin.' Steve Justice, TTSA's COO and formerly head of Advanced Systems at Lockheed Martin's Skunk Works, is quoted: 'The structure and composition of these materials are not from any known existing military or commercial application.' He adds the crucial custody caveat in the same paragraph: 'They've been collected from sources with varying levels of chain-of-custody documentation, so we are focusing on verifiable facts and working to develop independent scientific proof of the materials' properties and attributes.' And a dating claim: 'In some cases, the manufacturing technology required to fabricate the material is only now becoming available, but the material has been in documented possession since the mid-1990's.' Justice explicitly frames the endgame as transition to capability: 'If the claims associated with these assets can be validated and substantiated, then we can initiate work to transition them from being a technology to commercial and military capabilities.'

On 17 October 2019 TTSA announced a Cooperative Research and Development Agreement with the U.S. Army Combat Capabilities Development Command, sharing its work with the Ground Vehicle Systems Center and Ground Vehicle Survivability and Protection, with the Army providing laboratories, expertise and resources. The announced technology areas were material science, space-time metric engineering, quantum physics, beamed energy propulsion, and active camouflage. A CRADA is not a procurement; no money moved to TTSA and the Army acquired nothing. What it did establish is a formal, signed channel through which privately held claimed-exotic material could reach Army laboratories.

The analytic endgame arrived in 2024. Oak Ridge National Laboratory analyzed the magnesium-zinc-bismuth 'Art's Parts' sample; TTS posted the ORNL report in July of that year. A TTS-AARO-ORNL meeting was held on 27 August 2024. Puthoff and Eric W. Davis then produced their own analysis, submitted to the TTS Board of Directors on 27 September 2024 and published by TTS with a recommendation history attached. TTS's own summary of the outcome is unusually candid and is the single most useful sentence ADAM has produced: 'the Puthoff/Davis analysis (and the ORNL analysis) did not reveal any form of proof of exotic (Extraterrestrial, Ultra-terrestrial, or inter-dimensional) origin, but it did not absolutely rule it out either.' TTS adds that 'the actual purpose of the material, though clearly manufactured, still remains a bit of a mystery.'

AARO's 2024 Historical Record Report Volume 1 states more bluntly that a piece of metal alleged to be recovered from an off-world spacecraft was determined to be ordinary, of terrestrial origin, and to possess no exceptional qualities. Both statements can be reconciled: nothing exotic was demonstrated. What was demonstrated is process, a private entity holding claimed recovered material got it into a DOE national laboratory and into a room with the Pentagon's UAP office.

The scientific parallel track matters for method rather than result. Garry Nolan of Stanford, Jacques Vallée, Sizun Jiang and Larry Lemke published 'Improved instrumental techniques, including isotopic analysis, applicable to the characterization of unusual materials with potential relevance to aerospace forensics' in Progress in Aerospace Sciences volume 128 in January 2022, DOI 10.1016/j.paerosci.2021.100788. That paper is the peer-reviewed statement of what a defensible analysis of a claimed anomalous sample would look like, isotopic ratio analysis, SIMS, and comparison against known terrestrial baselines, and it is the standard against which every ADAM claim should be measured.

Who matters by name: Tom DeLonge (founder), Steve Justice (COO, ex-Skunk Works Advanced Systems), Hal Puthoff (EarthTech, TTSA VP Science and Technology, AAWSAP DIRD author), Eric W. Davis (AAWSAP DIRD author, co-author of the 2024 counter-analysis), Linda Moulton Howe (prior custodian), Luis Elizondo and Chris Mellon (TTSA principals in the founding period), Garry Nolan and Jacques Vallée on the independent scientific track. What the record shows is a documented chain of private acquisition, a signed Army CRADA, a national-laboratory analysis, and a published negative-but-not-exclusionary result. What the record does not show is any chain of custody back to a recovery event.

### What it has denied

AARO's public position, in its 2024 Historical Record Report Volume 1, is that a sample alleged to be from an off-world craft was found to be ordinary and terrestrial with no exceptional qualities. Notably, TTS itself does not dispute the negative finding; its published position is that neither ORNL nor its own analysts proved exotic origin, only that they did not absolutely rule it out. There is no live factual dispute over the ORNL result, the dispute is over interpretation of manufacture, purpose and provenance.

Roles the board assigns it: custody, reverse-engineering, research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Is the October 2019 Army CCDC CRADA text publicly available in full, and is it still in force?
- What exactly did AARO contribute at the 27 August 2024 TTS-ORNL meeting, and does AARO hold a record of it?
- TTS states that an additional 'specified scientific contributor' was to weigh in on the MgZn/Bi material but never did. Who was it?
- What is the documented chain of custody for the 'Art's Parts' sample prior to Linda Moulton Howe's possession in the mid-1990s?
- How many samples does ADAM hold in total, and have any others been analyzed at a federal or national laboratory?
- Did any Army laboratory produce a written report under the CRADA, and has it been requested under FOIA?
- Justice said the manufacturing technology for some material 'is only now becoming available.' Which specific fabrication process is being referenced, and by whose assessment?
- Were any ADAM materials or analyses offered to, or reviewed by, the House Oversight Committee?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 73 of 1066, score 69). 8 connections, 7 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C3/A1/D2.

# NO DATE: no start date recorded

## The 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents (DIRDs)

The 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents (DIRDs) is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates no dates recorded; axes C0 A0 D0; 41 connections; in the reverse-engineering band.

In one line: AAWSAP contract deliverable set, 2008-2010. A DOCUMENT SERIES, not a programme, the individual papers are wired to their authors and to AAWSAP, and this node is the shelf they sit on. Absorbed the duplicate node dird-collection on 14 Aug 2026.

### What it is

The Defense Intelligence Reference Documents are a series of approximately thirty-eight unclassified technical survey papers commissioned from outside specialists under the AAWSAP contract and published by the Defense Intelligence Agency between roughly 2009 and 2011. They cover metamaterials, warp drive, traversable wormholes, antigravity, negative mass propulsion, quantum vacuum energy extraction, invisibility cloaking, high-frequency gravitational wave communications, and human biological effects of anomalous fields. Thirty-seven of the thirty-eight were released to The Black Vault by DIA on 25 March 2022 under FOIA case FOIA-0159-2018.

### Why it is implicated

The DIRDs are the most literal artefact on this board: a list, written by the United States government's own defence intelligence agency, of every theoretical route by which the observed flight performance of anomalous objects might be achieved. Read as a set, they are not a scattershot survey but a systematic decomposition of a capability - lift without visible propulsion, translation in space and time, signature reduction, materials that do not behave conventionally, and the effects such a craft would have on a human body standing near it. The government commissioned this taxonomy in 2008 and 2009 while the same contract was running field investigation teams and proposing laboratories to examine recovered material. One of the thirty-eight, the classified SECRET version of 'State of the Art and Evolution of High-Energy Laser Weapons,' has never been released and was not even acknowledged as withheld in the release letter, which is why the requester appealed on the adequacy of the search rather than on an exemption.

### The longer account

There is a way of reading the DIRD series that makes it the single most informative document set on this board, and it is simply to read the titles in order and ask what question they collectively answer.

The released thirty-seven include 'Metallic Glasses - Status and Prospects for Aerospace Applications,' 'Aerospace Applications of Programmable Matter,' 'Materials for Advanced Aerospace Platforms,' 'Metamaterials for Aerospace Applications' and 'Metallic Spintronics' - the materials question. They include 'Positron Aerospace Propulsion,' 'Inertial Electrostatic Confinement Fusion,' 'Advanced Nuclear Propulsion For Manned Deep Space Missions,' 'Negative Mass Propulsion,' 'Aneutronic Fusion Propulsion,' 'Aneutronic Fusion Propulsion II,' 'MHD Air Breathing Propulsion and Power for Aerospace Applications' and 'Laser Lightcraft Nanosatellites' - the propulsion question. They include 'Warp Drive, Dark Energy, and the Manipulation of Extra Dimensions,' 'Advanced Space Propulsion Based on Vacuum (Spacetime Metric) Engineering,' 'Traversable Wormholes, Stargates, and Negative Energy,' 'The Role of Superconductors in Gravity Research,' 'Antigravity for Aerospace Applications,' 'Concepts for Extracting Energy From the Quantum Vacuum' and 'Quantum Tomography of Negative Energy States in the Vacuum' - the physics-you-would-need question. They include 'Invisibility Cloaking - Theory and Experiments' and 'Detection and High Resolution Tracking of Vehicles at Hypersonic Velocities' - signature and detection. And they include 'Anomalous Acute and Subacute Field Effects on Human Biological Tissues,' 'Biosensors and BioMEMS,' 'Biomaterials,' 'Technological Approaches to Controlling External Devices,' 'Cognitive Limits on Simultaneous Control of Multiple Unmanned Spacecraft' and 'Cockpits in the Era of Breakthrough Flight' - the human question, which is to say, what happens to a person near one of these, and what would it be like to be inside one.

That is not a random reading list. It maps one to one onto the twelve technical areas in the AAWSAP statement of objectives: lift, propulsion, control, power generation, spatial and temporal translation, materials, configuration and structure, signature reduction, human interface, human effects, armament, and peripheral areas. The DIRDs are the deliverable structure of the contract made visible. And two of the titles are difficult to explain by any framing other than the one this board starts from. 'Anomalous Acute and Subacute Field Effects on Human Biological Tissues' is a medical paper about injury from proximity to something. 'Cockpits in the Era of Breakthrough Flight' is a human factors paper about operating a vehicle that does not exist.

The release history is its own artefact. Eric Davis, who worked for Bigelow Aerospace and BAASS, said on Coast to Coast AM on 28 January 2018 that thirty-eight such reports had been produced under AAWSAP. Within forty-eight hours The Black Vault filed for all of them; the request became FOIA case FOIA-0159-2018. In January 2019 a listing breakdown of the reports arrived under a separate case, FOIA-0033-2019, though a slightly different version of the list had already leaked. DIA completed the main request more than four years later, on 25 March 2022, releasing thirty-seven of the thirty-eight. The one that did not come out was the SECRET-classified version of 'State of the Art and Evolution of High-Energy Laser Weapons.' Critically, it was not listed as withheld. The release letter did not reference it at all, which is why the appeal was filed on the adequacy of DIA's search rather than on the applicability of an exemption - a procedurally different and, for a board about concealment, a more interesting posture. An unclassified version of that same paper, DIRD 23, is in the released set at thirty-one pages.

The DIRDs also function as a personnel map. The authorship of the series connects the private technical layer catalogued elsewhere on this board directly to a DIA contract: the space-time metric engineering and quantum vacuum papers, the wormhole and warp drive papers, and the materials papers were written by named specialists who reappear later at To The Stars Academy, at EarthTech International, and in the disclosure hearings of 2023 to 2025. The government did not go to a federal laboratory or an FFRDC for this survey. It went to a small network of physicists with an existing interest in the subject, through a small business set-aside contract, performed at the contractor's own facility.

What the series does not contain is equally important and should be stated plainly. No DIRD reports an examination of recovered material. No DIRD describes a craft. They are literature surveys and theoretical assessments, and several of them conclude that the routes they examine are not presently achievable. AARO's characterisation is that they were 'exploratory papers addressing the 12 scientific areas tasked in the contract's statement of work' which 'were never thoroughly peer reviewed.' On the face of the documents, that is accurate.

The argument for their place on this map is therefore not that they contain the secret. It is that they define its shape. A government office that believed it was looking at nothing would not commission a paper on the acute and subacute effects of anomalous fields on human biological tissue. A programme whose only interest was foreign aerospace threat out to 2050 would not need a survey of traversable wormholes. The DIRDs are the negative space around an object: thirty-eight independent attempts to explain, from first principles, how something that had already been observed could possibly work.

### What it has denied

DIA has not denied the existence of the series; it released thirty-seven of thirty-eight papers under FOIA in March 2022. Its release documentation made no reference to the classified SECRET version of the high-energy laser weapons report, neither producing it nor logging it as withheld. AARO's position is that the papers are unreviewed exploratory surveys tasked under the contract's stated aerospace-threat scope.

Roles the board assigns it: reverse-engineering, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The status of the thirty-eighth document, the SECRET version of 'State of the Art and Evolution of High-Energy Laser Weapons,' and the outcome of the adequacy-of-search appeal.
- The internal DIA tasking record: who chose the thirty-eight topics, when, and against what requirement. No topic-selection memorandum has been released.
- Whether the DIRDs were circulated, cited or acted upon anywhere in DoD or the Intelligence Community after publication, or whether they were shelved on delivery.
- Whether any of the DIRD authors held or were offered special-access read-ins in connection with the work, which would connect the unclassified series to a classified effort.
- Whether the 'proposals to set up laboratories to examine any recovered UFO materials' that AARO says the contractor made are documented anywhere alongside the DIRD tasking, and whether any DIRD topic was selected in support of such a laboratory.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 9 of 1066, score 122). 41 connections, 38 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D0.

- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** AARO characterises the DIRDs as 'exploratory papers addressing the 12 scientific areas tasked in the contract's statement of work' and states that 'These scientific papers were never thoroughly peer reviewed.' Source: https://documents2.theblackvault.com/documents/dod/AARO_Historical_Record_Report_Volume_1_2024.pdf
- **Tier 2 named person on the record, allegation shaped wording as stored.** AARO states that the DIRDs represent essentially the whole of what AAWSAP produced, that it 'has yet to uncover any other substantive UAP case work conducted by AAWSAP/AATIP,' and that the remainder of the effort consisted of case reviews, interviews of observers, and work on alleged paranormal activity at the contractor principal's Utah property. Source: https://documents2.theblackvault.com/documents/dod/AARO_Historical_Record_Report_Volume_1_2024.pdf
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Eric Davis stated in a Coast to Coast AM interview on 28 January 2018 that while working for Bigelow Aerospace/BAASS he and colleagues created thirty-eight Defense Intelligence Reference Documents under AAWSAP, which is what triggered the FOIA request that eventually produced the release. Source: https://www.theblackvault.com/documentarchive/the-advanced-aerospace-weapon-system-applications-program-aawsap-documentation/

### Its strongest connections

- The 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents (DIRDs) comes in from **Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program (AAWSAP)** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: 26 FY2009 + 12 FY2010 technical reports. The DIRDs are the contract's deliverables. DIA Talking Paper U-973-09 records that the RFP broke the work into twelve technical areas - lift, propulsion, control, armament, signature reduction and the rest - and the Defense Intelligence Reference Documents are the products those areas were delivered as. Every one of the 32 series-member edges was verified against the released PDF's cover page on 22 August 2026. Sources: FedBizOpps notice: ADVANCED AEROSPACE WEAPON SYSTEM APPLICATIONS PROGRAM, Solicitation HHM40208R0211 (DIA FOIA release), https://www.dia.mil/FOIA/FOIA-Electronic-Reading-Room/FileId/237630/, dated 2008-08-18; DIA Info Memo U-10-2552 to USD(AT&L), 'Advanced Aerospace Threat and Identification Program' (DIA FOIA release), https://www.dia.mil/FOIA/FOIA-Electronic-Reading-Room/FileId/237626/, dated 2010.
- The 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents (DIRDs) comes in from **Eric W. Davis** (person, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: authored five of the 38 DIRDs. Items 8, 11, 14, 31 and 34, wormholes, antigravity, quantum vacuum energy, laser lightcraft and negative energy tomography. Sources: DIA letter to SASC, https://documents.theblackvault.com/documents/dia/dia-aatip-reports.pdf, dated 2018-01-09.
- The 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents (DIRDs) comes in from **Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies (BAASS), LLC** (institution, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: prime contractor that commissioned and delivered the 38 DIRDs. BAASS subcontracted the reports to EarthTech, Lockheed Martin, and a dozen universities and delivered them to DIA as Defense Intelligence Reference Documents. Sources: DIA letter to SASC, https://documents.theblackvault.com/documents/dia/dia-aatip-reports.pdf, dated 2018-01-09.
- The 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents (DIRDs) comes in from **Harold E. Puthoff** (person, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: authored DIRD on vacuum/spacetime metric engineering. Item 5 on DIA's transmittal list; the closest thing in the released set to a theory of how the reported craft operate. Sources: DIA letter to SASC, https://documents.theblackvault.com/documents/dia/dia-aatip-reports.pdf, dated 2018-01-09.
- The 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents (DIRDs) comes in from **EarthTech International / Institute for Advanced Studies at Austin** (institution, board tier A), a funding link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: largest single DIRD producer, six of 38. A small Austin firm produced more of the deliverable set than any university or defence prime, indicating a directed rather than competed distribution. Sources: The War Zone, https://www.twz.com/26056/heres-the-list-of-studies-the-militarys-secretive-ufo-program-funded-some-were-junk, dated 2019-01-18.
- The 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents (DIRDs) comes in from **Christopher C. 'Kit' Green** (person, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: authored the human biological effects DIRD. Item 12, 'Field Effects on Biological Tissues', released as 'Anomalous Acute and Subacute Field Effects on Human Biological Tissues', a government document about injuries.
